China’s Economics of Denial

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Oliver Melton is a Director with Rhodium Group’s China practice.

Logan Wright is a Partner at Rhodium Group and leads its research on China’s economy and financial markets. He is the author of the book Broken China: How the Economic Miracle Shattered and What it Means for the World.

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

China has correctly diagnosed its most pressing economic problems. The Chinese Communist Party has acknowledged that its economy relies too much on investment and exports and that persistently weak household consumption is the core threat to future growth. The leadership also knows that this imbalanced growth model is causing trade friction with developed and emerging economies alike and will continue to do so. They know there is a growing chorus of economists (including many quiet voices in China) raising the alarm that, if Beijing does not make major changes soon, China will face ever-slower growth, cascading trade disputes, and potentially stagnating household income.

But what is puzzling is that the country’s leaders are not acting. Beijing has announced only modest plans to boost household consumption and domestic demand, and its overall economic strategy remains essentially unchanged—focused on expanding investment in manufacturing, especially in advanced technologies and new strategic industries.

Theories abound as to why Beijing has chosen to ignore the warnings. Many speculate that Chinese leader Xi Jinping is not receiving accurate information or that he and his closest allies have abandoned development as a priority in favor of national security goals or a desire to control the commanding heights of global industry. Others argue that Beijing knows what it should do, but political or institutional constraints prevent it from taking decisive action.

In truth, while these factors all play a part in shaping policy, the real explanation is far simpler. The party is sticking to this path because the leadership believes it is working. It sees the same troubling data—including rising external surpluses, weak consumption, slower household income growth, weak credit demand, rising unemployment and underemployment, and persistent deflation—but it has reached wildly different conclusions about what these developments indicate and what Beijing should do in response.

Chinese economic policy is nested within a methodical planning process, which is meant to ensure that short-term pressures do not distract from long-term priorities. The problem is that the long-term strategy is predicated on the belief that control of advanced technologies and industry will trigger a new round of high-speed growth. This economic strategy can best be understood as a Marxism-infused misapplication of neoclassical economic development theory: Beijing is obsessed with control over technology and physical capital and believes that its ultrahigh levels of investment will accelerate productivity gains and wage growth. But a disproportionate focus on manufacturing and technology cannot increase productivity or wages if those investments are decoupled from the needs of the market and trends in aggregate final demand. This is especially true in a country as large as China—already the world’s largest exporter—that cannot rely on further exports to fuel growth.

China’s worldview treats development as equivalent to capital accumulation and sees productivity as determined by the control and deployment of technology. It posits that the growth of the service sector, higher wages, and higher consumption are the consequences of that physical underlying foundation—they follow naturally in later stages of the development process once the productive industries have flourished. In this conception of economic development, the right strategy is always more investment, more industry, and more technology. The role of the state is to ensure that household savings and industrial profits are recycled through the state-owned banking system or government-guided capital into priority investments rather than frivolous activities, such as services or consumption. The resulting structural imbalance that weakens household income growth, the service sector, and public services was thus not an accident; in China’s economic development theory, this is seen as self-discipline that will pay off with more rapid long-term development.

A key corollary to this view is that the government’s macroeconomic management must guide resource allocation proactively through hands-on intervention, not impassively through interest rates and exchange rates. The state can harness the dynamism and ambition of markets to promote efficiency and innovation in defined spaces, but it is the government’s job to identify the correct focal points for investment to ensure that priority sectors grow at maximum speed and then to intervene to ameliorate imbalances when they inevitably arise.

In this conception of macroeconomics, the economy consists of a massive input-output table where every cell in the matrix is a sector that consumes other cells’ outputs and provides inputs to other producers. If there is an imbalance in supply or demand for certain products—which becomes evident in high or low prices—it may be because of overinvestment in the producing sector or underinvestment in downstream consuming sectors. Government policy seeks to increase output and steadily lower prices by facilitating investment and production throughout the matrix, adjusting the pace and balance of resource allocation as needed if one cell’s output exceeds the demands of the others. The goal is not to develop overcapacity or low profitability in these targeted sectors, but the optimal path for rapid development will inevitably push sectors in that direction.

Beijing has followed this playbook closely. When, for instance, the real estate sector collapsed in 2022 and the structural imbalances in China’s economy became too big to ignore, Beijing responded by increasing its efforts to micromanage credit flows, reversing experiments with interest rate liberalization and financial market reforms while tightening oversight of capital markets and industrial policy funding. Central bank relending facilities now channel low-cost bank loans to prioritize industrial upgrading and equipment purchases for targeted sectors. Thousands of government-run equity funds provide low-cost capital to firms that support a web of industrial and technology strategies. State-owned enterprises intervene to help boost valuations in the stock market, and regulators prioritize firms in strategic sectors for new listings. When a sector is overinvested, policy campaigns such as “anti-involution”—a neologism that describes a basket of discordant policies to resolve overcapacity—are deployed to slow future investment, buttress profitability by fixing prices, or set standards to restrict market entry in a bid to slow output while the other cells in the input-output tables catch up and restore balance over time.

Although hard to comprehend in the context of a market economy, some aspects of this approach have an understandable internal logic in China, especially at a micro level. The collapse of the property sector—roughly a quarter of GDP at its peak—sent massive shock waves through very specific supply and demand pathways in the economy. It made sense for Beijing to calibrate policy for the affected sectors and think in terms of input-output tables as it designed its responses. The frequency of bubbles in China’s equity, commodity, and property markets over the past 15 years has left policymakers deeply wary of accommodative monetary policy for the entire economy, which helps explain why they are experimenting with more targeted tools aimed at specific sectors.

Likewise, a certain level of triumphalism in Beijing is understandable, given that Chinese companies are breaking into pole positions in major conventional industries, such as autos and telecommunications, and many others are poised to thrive in cutting-edge sectors, such as robotics and biotechnology—all in the wake of a massive hit to GDP. For a generation of economic policymakers who cut their teeth trying to revitalize a moribund planned economy in the 1990s and the first decade of this century, the emergence of world-leading (often private) firms is definitive evidence that Beijing has found a winning recipe. From this perspective, Beijing is also dismissive of foreign complaints about unfair market interventions or trade imbalances as the sour grapes of rich countries that had grown accustomed to rent-seeking technology monopolies.

China’s leaders also see some deflation as the fruits of hard-won efficiency gains: the elimination of bottlenecks, economies of scale in manufacturing clusters, construction of world-class infrastructure, improved logistics, and removal of market barriers that have lowered costs up and down manufacturing supply chains. One-off cost reductions from efficiency gains or the abrupt loss of construction activity must be digested by the economy, they would argue, not offset by stimulus. And while foreign commentators and dignitaries warn of growing trade pushback, the data tells a surprisingly different story: China’s export growth barely slowed as the Trump administration imposed sweeping duties and the European Union set up defenses against a handful of sectors, such as electric vehicles.

The core problem with China’s economic strategy, however, is that aggregate domestic demand consistently falls far short of domestic supply. Chinese planners’ input-output tables will not balance unless domestic workers are buying the goods that China’s industries produce. Otherwise, everything else must be exported, often at lower prices. The leadership understands this in principle but believes that the control of advanced industries and technology will, ipso facto, cause productivity to rise, sustain economic growth into the future, and generate higher wages and therefore higher consumption.

This is the flaw in Beijing’s approach—the dangerous legacy of vestigial Marxism and misapplied neoclassical growth theory. In reality, state intervention in markets can depress overall productivity and workers can see flat wages even when China succeeds at promoting advanced industries.

It is true that capital and technology create productivity gains in well-functioning market economies. But the causality usually breaks down if resources are allocated by policy interventions that are divorced from returns on investment. The control or ownership of advanced industries does not inherently cause economic growth, because they are far too small a share of the economy. Instead, new products or technologies generate productivity when they are distributed and used throughout the economy in ways that improve efficiency or increase output. It is not the success of a robotics firm that leads to efficiency gains, for example, it is the deployment of its robots in factories to save production costs—namely by displacing workers—and boost output. If those benefits do not exceed the true cost of the new industrial robots—even with layers of subsidies—then the net effect is not positive, no matter how impressive the factories themselves are, or how successful the robotics firm itself appears.

The use of new production technologies also requires a degree of creative destruction that Beijing’s own industrial policy interventions have inhibited. When governments, not price-conscious firms, make technology and investment decisions, policymakers are not just gambling that they know better than the market how to boost long-run performance. They are also betting that they know how to use those technologies to create innovative new business models. Yet in China, state influence over resource allocation has ossified markets, especially by reducing bankruptcies, in ways that limit the fruits of innovation and efficiency gains in the broader economy. Additionally, much of China’s industrial policy aims to build self-reliance, which, by definition, focuses on duplicative investments that buttress domestic industrial prowess without necessarily adding efficiency.

Moreover, even though China’s many layers of industrial policy have helped launch successful companies and built an impressive fleet of ultramodern factories, there are also tremendous inefficiencies and waste that never make the headlines. Overall productivity must be calculated comprehensively, evaluating the net output gains against all the industrial subsidies, bad debts, foregone profits, and indirect support for investment that the government has provided. The reality is that China’s aggregate success rate is far lower than the shining stars would suggest: the remarkable global reach of electric vehicle and battery makers such as BYD, CATL, and Li Auto masks the dozens of failed companies that generated nonperforming loans and ate up unprofitable capital subsidies throughout the bulk of the sector and its supply chain. It is far from obvious that the net return on investment from industrial policy spending, estimated by the International Monetary Fund to be up to four percent of GDP, is positive, and it could be deeply negative, despite pockets of success.

Finally, Beijing is betting that superefficient manufacturing and success in strategic industries will eventually lead to higher wages, which will then cause consumption to surge. It is true that productivity growth causes real incomes to rise in a competitive market economy. But if investments in labor-saving technologies are driven by state policy, not returns on investment, wages can fall even as firms become more efficient. Rising household income requires a key intermediate step: employment, which is not a core characteristic of China’s capital-intensive, advanced manufacturing investment strategy. Indeed, state funding has helped China—a middle-income economy—become a world leader in high-tech “dark factories” that operate without distracting human accommodations, such as light. This may increase output in prized manufacturing cells in the input-output tables, but if demand for labor is not growing in aggregate, the whole equation starts to fall apart.

Still, the most pernicious consequence of Beijing’s economic model is entrenched deflation. The focus on expanding production combined with China’s weak consumer demand has caused the producer price index to fall for longer than it has risen over the past decade. This is a far larger threat to China’s economy than a mere nuisance of sectoral imbalances. Deflation reduces private-sector credit demand; undercuts incentives for new investments; and crushes employment, wage expectations, and, therefore, consumer confidence, which leads to weaker domestic demand—not in carefully demarcated input-output relationships but throughout the economy. Deflation also saps fiscal revenues and puts pressure on financial institutions in ways that severely limit the state’s capacity to act decisively the longer it lasts. Deflationary pressures in China also spill over to the global economy through cheaper exports and a weakening real exchange rate, driving more tensions between Beijing and its trading partners.

There is immense risk in falling behind the curve in fighting deflation, but Beijing is not likely to change course if it continues to believe its strategy will boost productivity and jumpstart domestic demand in the long run. Some analysts speculate that Beijing could be forced into a reappraisal of its vision in response to unemployment pressures or the proliferation of foreign trade friction, but it has already shown its cards. The accumulation of unemployment, underemployment, and low wages for Chinese households and workers are already significant, yet Beijing has responded with only modest new household subsidies and incremental investment in social services. Likewise, as external trade tensions have risen, Beijing has managed these country-by-country disputes as diplomatic issues, through a mix of threats and negotiations, to preserve the bulk of China’s export markets.

The decision to pursue short-term Band-Aid measures for a weak labor market and foreign trade tensions is confusing at first pass. For a government laser-focused on self-reliance and eliminating foreign dependencies, it is a curious strategy to become ever more dependent on foreign demand—over which Beijing has little control—as its primary driver of economic growth. Likewise, for a regime that is obsessed with social stability, it would seem prudent to focus more on wages and labor demand. Yet the strategy makes a degree of sense when viewed from the leadership’s perspective that China is in a transitional period. It may be unpleasant and difficult, but it is an unavoidable step on the path to a technology-led growth model.

The leadership’s current confidence in this transition process means that it could be many years before the warning signs, already visible, become persuasive internally. This all but guarantees that Beijing will continue to defer action while deflationary pressures and external trade tensions build. Deflation and weak corporate profitability are already eroding China’s fiscal revenues, which have basically been flat in nominal terms since 2019. Deflation and the growing debt burden are also corroding the financial system, which will ultimately require large write-offs and recapitalization from the government budget to prevent credit growth from slowing further. A $54 billion bank recapitalization just took place two months ago, with little effect on lending.

Beijing’s ideological commitment to its industry- and technology-focused development strategy is creating a dangerous cycle that will likely produce weaker consumption, slower income growth, and even greater trade friction with the rest of the world. Moreover, in the face of such challenges, China’s economic leadership is compensating by expanding state intervention in the economy in ways that further undermine market forces and the independence of the private sector. At this rate, even if the leadership eventually recognizes the need to change strategy, China may not have the fiscal or financial space to rebalance the economy toward a more sustainable and diversified growth model.

Kyle Shanahan confirms Jauan Jennings decided not to pursue trade back to 49ers

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Yes, the 49ers tried to arrange a reunion with receiver Jauan Jennings.

Over the weekend, Jay Glazer of Fox Sports reported that the 49ers and Vikings had a deal in place, but that Jennings eventually decided to stay in Minnesota. On Wednesday, 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan confirmed it.

“The report was accurate, just not totally exactly detailed up,” Shanahan told reporters. “I mean, you can’t talk to other players on other teams. You’re not allowed to. We love Jauan. We have such a good relationship with Jauan and know him really well and we didn’t want to do it without talking to him first. So, we had to agree on some things to at least make it close enough to be able to talk to him.

“Then when we got on a FaceTime with J.J., it went well. It was awesome. We talked about a lot of stuff, and he ended up saying that he was happy with where he was. It wasn’t just like a definite ‘no’ first, but he was like, ‘Man, Kyle, I’m pretty happy here and liking stuff.’ And when I heard the way he talked and where we’re at, I agreed with him. So, it was a good conversation and something that was possible, but that’s why it didn’t go down.”

Jennings still hasn’t done much for the Vikings. In six games, he has six catches for 54 yards.

The fact that the Vikings allowed Jennings to talk to the 49ers shows that the Vikings were willing to move on. The fact that Jennings stayed has kept receiver Odell Beckham Jr. parked on the Minnesota practice squad.

The 50 Best Restaurants 2026: The Full List of Winners

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Congratulations to Asador Etxebarri in Spain, named the World’s Best Restaurant of 2026

Tonight, the 50 Best Restaurants 2026 were announced at a ceremony in Lima, Peru, with Basque Country restaurant Asador Etxebarri crowned the new top slot on the list. Bangkok tasting counter Gaggan was ranked No. 2, Copenhagen’s immersive, 50-course experience Alchemist was No. 3, Mexico City’s Quintonil No. 4, and Hong Kong’s Wing No. 5, shuffling most of the top rankings from last year. New York City’s Atomix was the only U.S. restaurant to make the 50 Best Restaurants.

Of the specialty awards, Singapore’s Lesley Liu received the Best Sommelier Award (sponsored by Vik), Ghana Food Movement was given the Champions of Change (sponsored by illycaffe), Yiaga in Melbourne was named the One to Watch, Doris Lam received the 50 Best Restaurants Scholarship Award, Jessica Rosval of Al Gatto Verde was named Best Female Chef (sponsored by Lexus), Thailand-based Dej Kewkacha was Best Pastry Chef (sponsored by Sosa), Atomix’s Junghyun Park was given the Estrella Damm Chefs’ Choice Award, and Ferran Adrià was given the SevenRooms Icon Award.

Just as a review, U.K.-based media company William Reed launched the 50 Best Restaurants lists in the early 2000s, publishing the first World’s 50 Best Restaurants list in Restaurant magazine in 2002. The 50 Best gained greater fame in the 2010s, with the top spot going to the likes of Noma (Copenhagen, Norway), El Celler de Can Roca (Girona, Spain), and Osteria Francescana (Modena, Italy). The organization made the wise decision in 2019 to move each year’s winner to a separate “Best of the Best” list — a sort of hall of fame for takers of the top spot — giving new restaurants the opportunity to take the crown. Since then, Geranium in Copenhagen (2022), Disfrutar in Spain (2024), Central (2023), and Maido (2025) in Lima have earned the No. 1 ranking. Noma qualified as a winner again in 2021 because it had “closed and reopened.”

The 50 has also expanded its reach with multiple regional lists, such as the Latin America’s 50 Best, Middle East and North Africa’s 50 Best, Asia’s 50 Best, the 50 Best Bars (which also has its own regional lists), 50 Best Hotels, and even 50 Best Wineries. The entire brand has reached a level of global reputation and fungibility that rivals something like the Michelin Guide ( which has itself ventured into hotels and wineries). But the 50 Best Restaurants is the granddaddy of all listicles, and the one that draws the most attention and criticism regarding its methodology and alleged pay to play. One vocal critic, chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, announced earlier this year that she would not attend the ceremony, a move commenters like Top Chef judge Tom Colicchio lauded.

The polling is conducted anonymously among more than 1,100 voters, a mix of industry professionals, food writers, and “well-traveled gourmets,” with at least 25 percent turning over annually. Each voter nominates 10 restaurants in order of preference, up to six in their home region and at least four outside it. The rankings are just a pure tabulation of those votes.

The organization has made efforts to clarify its voting and ranking policies, stating that any restaurant can qualify and that what constitutes “best” depends on each voter. The 50 has also expanded its awards to consider diversity and inclusion, recognizing female chefs (which itself has raised eyebrows), sustainability, sommeliers, pastry chefs, hospitality, and Champions of Change, an award that “celebrates and recognizes unsung heroes” who are “driving positive change in their communities.”

Last week, the “back half” of the list, also known as 51-100, was announced, with the only U.S. restaurant, SingleThread (Healdsburg, California), ranked at No. 93. On the 2025 50 Best Restaurant list, New York’s Atomix was the only U.S. restaurant to make the top 50, ranked at 12. Perhaps in order to remedy the dearth of U.S. and Canadian restaurants on the World’s list, the 50 (the organization previously known as 50 Best), commenced North America’s 50 Best Restaurants in the fall of 2025. Atomix topped that list, followed by Mon Lapin (Montreal), Restaurant Pearl Morissette (Lincoln, Ontario), and other U.S.- and Canada-based restaurants.

Inside the chip talent war: Trade secrets and rising Chinese rivals

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A former Samsung Electronics employee who later joined Chinese memory chipmaker ChangXin Memory Technologies, or CXMT, was reportedly sentenced by the Seoul High Court on Oct. 22 to five years in prison for leaking Samsung's technology.

The sentence was reduced from seven years at his first trial in April. The former employee had joined CXMT in 2016.

The case highlights an intensifying battle over semiconductor know-how as Chinese chipmakers narrow the gap with established rivals, putting the race for top engineering talent increasingly at odds with efforts to protect trade secrets.

In 2023, a South Korean court granted Samsung's request to block a former DRAM researcher from joining U.S. rival Micron. It ruled that the two-year non-compete was not excessive and was necessary to protect Samsung's technology and business information, JoongAng Ilbo reported.

A decade ago, Chinese memory chipmakers were not Samsung's biggest concern when it came to losing experienced engineers.

Instead, the South Korean company was more focused on employees leaving for established rivals such as SK Hynix and Micron, according to a former employee who asked not to be identified because the information was private.

Samsung has been monitoring where certain departing employees went — to signal to remaining staff that moving to competitors would be difficult — according to a former employee.

The company also used non-compete agreements restricting employees from joining rivals for two years, as part of their employment contracts, with senior employees holding valuable technical expertise likely facing closer scrutiny.

But Samsung's concern extended beyond individual departures.

"It wasn't just about one employee leaving. The company wanted to send a message to those who stayed that moving to a competitor wouldn't be easy," the former employee said.

Meanwhile, Chinese semiconductor companies have sought to close the technology gap by recruiting experienced overseas engineers with high salaries, stock options, housing and family support, according to Jeongku Choi, a senior analyst at Counterpoint Research.

Their expertise has helped Chinese chipmakers overcome technical bottlenecks, particularly as U.S. export restrictions have limited China's access to advanced chipmaking equipment, Choi said.

Once viewed as unlikely to catch up with established players, Chinese manufacturers are now seen as "meaningful competitors," she added.

Counterpoint's Memory Tracker for the second quarter of 2026 projected CXMT's share of global DRAM revenue would reach 10%.

Established chipmakers are strengthening legal protections and internal security measures while adjusting compensation packages to retain key engineers, according to Choi.

South Korea has also designated certain semiconductor manufacturing technologies as "National Core Technologies," raising the penalties for unauthorized leaks of those technologies, Choi said.

The legal battle could intensify as Chinese memory companies expand beyond their home market.

"I believe that this management of trade secrets will expand its scope even further to IP and legal areas once Chinese memory companies move beyond the Chinese market and begin to expand their exports," Choi said.

The growing competition is putting greater focus on how semiconductor companies protect the knowledge accumulated by engineers working on complex manufacturing processes.

In Taiwan, home to the world's largest contract chipmaker TSMC, protections for trade secrets have evolved alongside the semiconductor industry’s growing strategic importance.

Jamie J. Yang, a partner specializing in antitrust and intellectual property at Innovatus Law, said Taiwan limits post-employment non-compete agreements to two years and requires employers to compensate former employees at least half their average monthly salary during the restricted period.

Yang added that the complexity of semiconductor manufacturing makes it difficult for any single engineer to possess enough knowledge to replicate an entire production line elsewhere.

"There is just very little chance that one person ... will know enough to replicate that line in any of the other companies," Yang said.

Moves by engineers between rival semiconductor companies could increase modestly as competition for top talent intensifies, while trade secret litigation is also likely to rise as firms seek to protect their technological edge, she added.

In Singapore, Zechariah Chan, a partner at law firm Lee & Lee, said protecting trade secrets can be particularly difficult in the semiconductor industry, where proprietary knowledge often involves intangible manufacturing techniques and methods.

Proving that a competitor has used proprietary manufacturing techniques can also be difficult, as outsiders generally see only the final product, according to Chan.

"There is no bright line between an engineer's expertise and proprietary information," he said. General skills and knowledge stay with the engineer, while trade secrets remain with the company.

In Singapore, non-compete clauses are enforceable only if they protect legitimate business interests and are reasonable in scope, geographical area and duration.

In Hong Kong, courts generally take a restrictive approach to non-compete clauses, enforcing them only when they protect legitimate business interests and go no further than necessary, according to Jezamine Fewins, head of litigation at Lewis Silkin in Hong Kong.

Courts are more willing to protect trade secrets through court injunctions, Fewins said.

"The difficulty is that often the trade secret is carried in the employee's head," she added. Information is more likely to qualify as a trade secret if its disclosure to a competitor could cause significant harm to the former employer and the company has taken steps to restrict access.

For chipmakers, Fewins said the key is to clearly define what constitutes a trade secret, make employees aware of that information and put safeguards in place to limit access.

Samsung and CXMT did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Google is expanding UCP-powered direct checkout in AI Mode and the Gemini app to Canadian shoppers, with a Merchant Center integration planned for next year.

Google is expanding direct checkout powered by the Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) to shoppers in Canada across AI Mode in Google Search and the Gemini app.

Sabrina Geremia, Vice President and Country Managing Director at Google Canada, announced the rollout on November 4, 2026.

The feature allows Canadian shoppers to purchase directly from eligible retailers while researching products on Google’s AI surfaces.

Google also announced plans to bring simplified UCP integration directly into Merchant Center starting next year.

Here’s what’s changing with the Canadian rollout, how the checkout flow works, and what retailers managing Merchant Center feeds should prepare for.

According to Google, the initial Canadian launch includes four partners and merchant groups:

These partnerships allow Canadian shoppers to complete purchases inside AI Mode in Google Search and the Gemini app without leaving the conversation.

During checkout, shoppers pay with Google Pay using the payment methods and shipping details saved in Google Wallet.

Google says retailers remain the seller of record and can customize the integration to fit their business needs.

In the coming months, Google plans to work with retailers to add more capabilities to the checkout experience, such as applying loyalty rewards.

For retailers outside the initial launch group, broader access is tied to an upcoming Merchant Center update.

Starting next year, Google says it will make simplified UCP integration available directly inside Merchant Center.

This update will allow retailers to enable direct checkout and other capabilities for their product listings across AI Mode in Google Search and the Gemini app directly from their accounts.

Google launched UCP earlier this year alongside industry partners as an open standard for agentic commerce.

The protocol creates a shared language so AI agents, consumer surfaces, businesses, and payment providers can work together across product discovery, purchasing, and post-purchase support without building custom connections for every platform.

In the announcement, Mani Fazeli, VP of Product at Shopify, stated:

Canadians are already asking AI what to buy, and now Shopify merchants selling into Canada can be faithfully represented and bought from directly in AI Mode and the Gemini app, with zero extra work. UCP handles the complexity of agentic commerce underneath, so shoppers can buy what they find without leaving the conversation.

While Fazeli notes that Shopify merchants selling into Canada can be bought from directly with zero extra work, Google specifies that the initial rollout applies to select Shopify merchants.

Direct checkout in AI Mode and the Gemini app keeps more of the transaction inside Google’s interfaces while keeping the merchant as the seller of record.

Even before the Merchant Center UCP integration arrives next year, product feed quality remains the foundation for visibility across Google’s shopping and AI surfaces.

Google’s generative AI optimization guide states that generative AI responses can include product listings and product information where appropriate. Using Merchant Center feeds can help products appear in both AI responses and standard Google Search results.

For ecommerce teams and agencies managing Canadian accounts, here’s a few suggestions to help you stay ahead of what’s coming:

Keeping your product feeds clean now will make it easier to adopt the Merchant Center UCP settings once they become available.

Google hasn’t yet shared a specific release date next year for the simplified UCP integration in Merchant Center, or disclosed eligibility criteria and onboarding steps for Canadian retailers outside the initial launch group. It also remains unclear how UCP direct checkout orders are reported or attributed in Merchant Center and Google Ads, or how direct checkout interacts with paid Shopping and Performance Max campaigns.

We’ll update this article as Google shares additional details about Merchant Center rollout timing and broader availability.

Kellie Pickler Has Settlement With Late Husband Kyle Jacobs’ Parents

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Kellie Pickler reached a settlement with the parents of her late husband Kyle Jacobs after they accused her of refusing to hand over his property—including guns and jewelry—following his 2023 death.

Kellie Pickler is putting a tough chapter behind her.

The "Red High Heels" singer reached a property settlement with her late husband Kyle Jacobs’ parents, Reed and Sharon, following his 2023 death, according to documents obtained by People on Nov. 4.

The terms of the settlement are confidential, though the docs stated that Pickler, 40, and her in-laws “have fully resolved all claims, disputes, and requests for relief by and between them" and that the parties are “satisfied with the distribution of assets” of his estate.

After Jacobs died by suicide at age 49, Pickler and his parents became engrossed in a legal battle over his property. His parents alleged that Pickler refused to give them Jacobs' belongings—such as his guns, jewelry and musical instruments—according to May 2024 documents obtained by People.

Among the items they requested were his Japanese samurai sword, Rolex watch, Garmin watch, baseball card albums, school awards, seven pistols, work laptop and iPhone, per their subpoena obtained by InTouch.

As for the American Idol alum, she said her in-laws "entered [her] home and obtained items of personal property after [Jacobs'] death," according to court docs obtained by the outlet.

Jacobs, a songwriter who worked with Garth Brooks and Tim McGraw, was found dead in the Nashville home he shared with Pickler in February 2023, with the Davidson Country Medical Examiner later ruling it a suicide.

Toxicology tests determined Jacobs did not have any drugs in his system at the time, though it noted he had a history of "pseudoseizures, gastrointestinal bleeding, elevated liver enzymes, and chronic alcohol use," per an autopsy report obtained by Taste of Country.

Pickler remained out of the spotlight in the wake of his passing, breaking her silence on her grief six months later.

"One of the most beautiful lessons my husband taught me was in a moment of a crisis, if you don't know what to do, ‘do nothing, just be still,'" the country star told People in August 2023. "I have chosen to heed his advice."

"Thank you to my family, friends, and supporters, for the countless letters, calls, and messages that you have sent my way," she continued. "It has truly touched my soul and it's helping me get through the darkest time in my life. As many of you have told me, you are all in my prayers."

The couple—who began dating in 2008 and married in 2011—weren't just partners in life, but also in music. They collaborated on several songs together, including 2008’s "One Last Time," 2013’s "The Woman I Am" and 2015’s "Feeling Tonight.”

"Yes, it's true, I'm sleeping with my producer!" she joked to People in 2015. "He is so good at what he does. I'm so excited to be in the studio again recording a new album, and working with Kyle has been amazing. He has watched me perform for years, so it was great to have a front row seat as an artist and see him in his element."

The "Best Days of Your Life" artist also credited Jacobs as her “saving grace.”

"My grandmother really showed me how to be a wife, a good wife," she told CMT Radio in 2011, per The Boot. "I never thought I'd be one though, because I never thought there would be a man out there who could love me. There's more flaws than smooth edges. They're mostly jagged, tattered. I'm really tattered at the seams. I'm very blessed."

SpY installs giant black triangle in madrid square to explore urban surveillance

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

the 20-meter black triangular intervention is equipped with concealed viewing apertures and apex-mounted CCTV cameras that return fragmented live feeds of the surrounding plaza.

SpY edited by christina vergopoulou November 5, 2026 3 min

Installed in Madrid’s Plaza de Cibeles directly fronting the Palacio de Cibeles, PANOPTICON by urban artist SpY is a large-scale temporary architectural installation configured as an equilateral black triangle measuring 20 meters on each side. The dark, monolithic geometry introduces an abrupt formal contrast against the ornate historic facade of the Palacio, directly intercepting established sightlines across the civic square.

The installation by Spanish urban artist SpY operates through an interplay of physical obstruction and mediated vision. While the opaque triangular volume physically blocks panoramic views of the urban context, discreet viewing apertures integrated along its perimeter invite visitors to peer inside. Each aperture is fitted with a display monitor wired to closed-circuit television (CCTV) cameras mounted at the apex of the structure. Through these video feeds, fragmented live footage of surrounding traffic, pedestrians, and adjacent architecture is projected back to the viewer from elevated overhead vantage points. By positioning the observer to look through surveillance feeds of the surrounding public space, the installation engages Jeremy Bentham’s architectural panopticon model, examining how digital tracking systems, public observation, and camera mediation govern movement and privacy in contemporary cities.

PANOPTICON is a large-scale public art installation designed by SpY in Madrid, Spainthe installation occupies the center of Plaza de Cibeles, facing the historic Palacio de Cibelesthe temporary structure takes the form of an equilateral triangle measuring 20 meters per side

Liam Neeson Reacts To Meryl Streep Taking Over ‘Narnia’ Role Of Aslan: “Nobody Better”

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After 16 years, Liam Neeson is passing one of literary’s great characters on to one of Hollywood’s greatest talents.

The Oscar nominee, who voiced Aslan in Disney’s The Chronicles of Narnia movies (2005-’10), recently shared his reaction to the news of Greta Gerwig’s Narnia: The Magician’s Nephew, premiering in Feb. 12 in theaters, giving Meryl Streep his seal of approval in taking over his former role.

“I’m sorry, this is news to me,” admitted Neeson when a ScreenRant reporter brought up the new adaptation of C.S. Lewis’ Chronicles of Narnia books.

When Neeson learned that Streep is taking over the role of the great talking lion Aslan, who is responsible for Narnia’s creation and depicted as the fictional land’s Jesus, he said, “Meryl is playing Jesus Christ? Well, if you’re going to make Christ a lady, nobody better than Meryl.” Watch on Deadline

Gerwig, who wrote and directed the upcoming adaptation, explained that the “list is not a long one” for actors she had in mind to voice the character. “I wanted someone who was profound but not pretentious, who had gravitas but not morose self-seriousness. Someone who could communicate depth and pathos as well as joy and delight,” she told Empire.

Watch Liam Neeson learn about Meryl Streep voicing Aslan in Greta Gerwig's new Narnia adaptation in real time! 🦁 pic.twitter.com/fgPmvtyPE8

“I wanted someone who was older than 70, so there was a wisdom of experience, but who also had the capacity of fresh wonder, like a child,” added Gerwig. “And also to be one of the greatest actors to ever do it. That list is not a long one.”

Based on Lewis’ sixth in the series, The Magician’s Nephew is chronologically the first in The Chronicles of Narnia, following Aslan’s creation of Narnia, a thousand years before the events of The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe.

In 2023, Gerwig confirmed her plans to adapt Lewis’ Chronicles of Narnia books for Netflix. Streep became attached to the role of Aslan last April.

Neeson previously voiced Aslan in The Chronicles of Narnia: The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe (2005), The Chronicles of Narnia: Prince Caspian (2008) and The Chronicles of Narnia: The Voyage of the Dawn Treader (2010).

James Gunn Approves Fan-Made Timeline Showing How Clayface And Blue Beetle Fit In The DC Universe

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

"Clayface" just expanded the DC Universe, giving fans their first introduction to the franchise's live-action Gotham City. The street-level story proved this still-fledgling cinematic enterprise can encompass an R-rated body horror just as easily as a charming crowd-pleaser like 2026's "Superman." Meanwhile, there are plenty of upcoming DCU movies that aim to similarly expand James Gunn and Peter Safran's shared universe. While the DC Studios co-heads are aiming to avoid the complexity of the erstwhile Snyderverse, you'd be forgiven for losing track of the new in-universe timeline. Now, we have an unofficial attempt to make sense of that very thing — and it's just been co-signed by Gunn himself.

User comicbookcollide posted a list of DC Universe projects on Threads in an attempt to arrange the various films and TV series into a coherent in-universe timeline. Turns out, they were pretty much on the money. Gunn responded to the post, writing, "Timelines give me anxiety, but I think this is pretty good." That's about as solid an endorsement for a fan-made timeline as you could hope for — though it should be noted that Gunn stopped short of fully endorsing the post as fact.

This particular timeline of events slots "Clayface" in as the second DCU event/project, listing the events therein as having occurred in 2021. Meanwhile, comicbookcollide also included "Blue Beetle," the 2023 film which belonged to the now-shuttered DC Extended Universe. This particular film, which Gunn has confirmed is canon to the DCU, is listed as having occurred in 2023 just prior to the events of "Creature Commandos" Season 1 — the official first DCU project. That also puts "Blue Beetle" before "Superman" and after the events of "Clayface," "The Suicide Squad," and "Peacemaker" Season 1.

James Gunn was never going to confirm a fan-made DC Universe timeline as 100% accurate and official. But there might be more to his reluctance to fully endorse comicbookcollide's post. While Gunn has said "Blue Beetle" is canon to the DCU, he also made clear there are a few retcons required to make it fit. The same is true of "Peacemaker" Season 1, which originally debuted while the DC Extended Universe was still running. The show received a particularly conspicuous retcon when Gunn and his DC Studios cohorts re-edited the Season 1 finale to include the Justice Gang rather than the former Justice League. Perhaps the fact that "Blue Beetle" isn't fully canon (but still sort-of is) accounts for why Gunn hasn't fully endorsed the fan-made timeline.

The Wrap previously reported that Jaime Reyes/Blue Beetle actor Xolo Maridueña is set to appear in Gunn's "Superman" sequel "Man of Tomorrow," which would fully solidify his original film as having been retconned into the DCU. But there's no doubt Gunn's approach is a little confusing.

Rather than rebooting DC's cinematic universe entirely, the director has tried to keep aspects of the former DCEU while charting a new course. Thus far, that hasn't been too much of an issue, but this is exactly the kind of esoteric minutiae that can turn off mass audiences and lead to widespread so-called superhero fatigue. As such, it is at least reassuring to see all the various DCU projects laid out in a timeline that Gunn has mostly co-signed, even if it all seemed a bit unnecessary from the beginning.

While comicbookcollide's timeline is fairly comprehensive, it does leave out some DCU events. For example, "Lanterns" Episode 1 began by clarifying the DC Universe timeline, revealing that Earth's first Lantern, Hal Jordan (Kyle Chandler), emerged in 1986 and revealed his identity in 1996. This means he had been operating long before the events of "Superman," which took place in 2025. These small but significant developments aren't included in the list posted to Threads, which otherwise does a solid job of making sense of the DCU timeline and combining former DCEU projects (such as the aforementioned "Peacemaker" Season 1, "The Suicide Squad," and "Blue Beetle") into the new chronology.

Meanwhile, Gunn's comment about timelines giving him "anxiety" seems a little baffling. It's his job to stay on top of this stuff and build a shared cinematic universe that maintains a coherent timeline and structure — otherwise DC would just stick with making the so-called "Elseworlds" projects and not worry about the whole interconnected aspect. He also recently appeared on a special "The Suicide Squad: Is It Canon?" podcast, a show specifically designed to clarify how the 2021 movie slots into the DCU and which parts are officially canon. Clearly, Gunn is thinking about these things and has a good idea of how these earlier films mesh with his new vision, to the extent it seems like he enjoys being the only one who really understands it all. Where the anxiety comes from, I'm not sure.

For now, we at least have a better idea of how "Blue Beetle" and "Clayface" fit into the overall plan, and it will be interesting to see how the DCU continues to expand in the coming years.

Joe Miñoso Talks Cruz’s Exit From ‘Chicago Fire,’ Storylines That Almost Happened & Plans To Direct

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

SPOILER ALERT: The following reveals major plot points from tonight’s episode of NBC‘s Chicago Fire.

NBC’s Chicago Fire celebrated its landmark 300th episode tonight, and it was bittersweet. While there was a lot to celebrate, it also marked Day 1’er Joe Miñoso’s final episode in the firefighter drama after 14+ years portraying the beloved Joe Cruz. And even though the character may have moved on to Minnesota with his family, Miñoso has been shadowing directors on set with the goal of directing an upcoming yet-to-be-determined episode.

“It was ultimately an appropriately timed choice. I think that both the team and I knew it in one way or the other. And when the time came, it just kind of felt like, yeah. You know, 300 is such a good number. It is such a gift to say that I did five of these episodes, let alone 300-plus across the three shows. It was a bittersweet moment,” Miñoso told Deadline earlier this week. Related Stories News 'Worth' Detective Drama From Mike Diliberti, Jake Coburn & Jack Carr In Development At NBC Late Night TV 'SNL' Pays Tribute To Patrick Stewart With Clip From His 1994 Episode

“An hour after I found out, I heard there was an opportunity to direct if I wanted it. So it was very whiplashy. It was such an insane back-and-forth, but at the same time, it just felt very right. I’m excited to grow into the next chapter. I’ve been directing and producing for a while, some of my own stuff. So it just felt like a natural evolution,” he added. Watch on Deadline

Learn more about Cruz’s fate in Deadline’s recap of Episode 5, aptly titled “Three Hundred.” Below, read more about our chat with Miñoso.

DEADLINE: Cruz is usually the fun guy, so it was tough to watch him struggle this season. What was it like playing a different side of this character?

JOE MIÑOSO: It was a welcome challenge. I appreciate everything that these writers have thrown at me over the course of the last 14 and a half seasons. It has always been a surprise and a joy. Everything has its different difficulty level. I think back to the longest one-er we’ve done on this show, which involved Chloe [Kristen Gutoskie] in a crash and me kind of running through this giant wreckage. That was, in its own way, a tremendous experience as a performer, especially working in film. You know the elevator episode with David Eigenberg, which was us in an elevator. We filmed 30 pages a day, which is unheard of. It was like being in a play. There are so many things to be proud of, and so many moments that I feel like I’ve learned so much and I’ve grown so much, and that’s all due to the show.

DEADLINE: There was a powerful scene at the end of this episode where Cruz and Severide (Taylor Kinney) have a 1:1 conversation. What can you share about shooting those scenes with him?

MIÑOSO: It will forever be my favorite scene that I’ve ever had with him. I don’t think we’ve really had the opportunity to explore these two men on equal footing in a really intimate way ever. In general, Severide is a man of few words. He’s a stoic human being who doesn’t really share much of what he’s going through. We’ve seen Kidd (Miranda Rae Mayo) over the years chip away at that wall. But he was so vulnerable in that scene. So many of the things we were saying could have very easily been Joe and Taylor talking in terms of our history together on the show. And it was, and will forever remain. I’m really proud that we had the conversation in general, and we got to see a different dynamic between the two.

DEADLINE: Joe’s storyline this season felt very much like, if something major didn’t stop him in his tracks, he wouldn’t have quit 51. Do you agree that sometimes life has to make us uncomfortable before we realize that it’s time to move on?

MIÑOSO: Yeah, I agree with you. The decision was made for him when he saw that the family had reached a new level and that he was needed in a different way. What’s so brilliant about that is that the first real storyline we see for Cruz is him possibly sacrificing his entire life and livelihood for the sake of his brother by leaving a gang member to burn in a building. Technically, he gives up and decides to leave the fire department because he thinks he can’t get over his own guilt over what he’s done. It takes one of his brothers, Mouch [Christian Stolte], to convince him otherwise. So to go from that to a man who is willing to give up his identity after all these years, ultimately, for the sake of his family, feels like we’re kind of at the beginning. He’s just grown into a different position.

DEADLINE: As a fan, things were pretty touch and go. We were scared they would kill off Cruz.

MIÑOSO: That was discussed. That was one of the options that was thrown out there. I won’t say by who, but yeah, that was pitched. But to [showrunner] Victor [Teran’s] and the writers’ credit, they decided that they’d rather go through this more emotional arc. I was honored by that because it was a big responsibility. It’s a large weight to carry, and I’m so glad they trusted me.

DEADLINE: That final moment when Cruz visits Otis’ [Yuri Sardarov] memorial was very emotional for me as a viewer. What did that scene mean to you?

MIÑOSO: That scene was actually my suggestion. There’s so much for the writers to think about when they’re writing for 9k characters and 20 million plot points to deal with. A lot can go under the radar. I just felt like we really needed to put a period on that relationship. They’re so intertwined, and he has still been such an integral part of Cruz’s life. It’s so obvious that Cruz is cleaning this memorial all the time. Now he’s passing those duties on.

The fact that the last words that Yuri says on the show are “I will be there with you always, brother,” and the opportunity to reflect on that felt like something that would not only honor the character but also the fans that have been with us for so long. I fell in love with those two as a team. That scene was hard to film. As I told the director, I want this to feel hopeful. I want this to be about the love of these two brothers, and I don’t want it to be sad. I don’t know that I’m going to be able to get a take without crying, because it was just so loaded. Yuri and I are still remarkable friends. We’re talking now more than ever because we’re in the middle of trying to get this rewatch podcast off the ground.

MIÑOSO: It’s brand new. We’re really just in the beginning phases of seeing if we can make something work. We just want to be able to celebrate the show and the friendship that we’ve built for the world.

DEADLINE: Did you tell Yuri about Otis being represented in your final episode?

MIÑOSO: Yes, I told him that there would be a moment. We were actually hoping that we could figure out how he could come back. But it felt right. I think this gives you the opportunity to miss Otis that much more. There was a moment where I maybe threw something out like [Otis] is the guy that gets Cruz out of the burning building. We had explored something like that earlier last year during our Halloween episode, where Cruz comes across this random old dude who is never seen again, and we allude to the fact that he was a ghost.

DEADLINE: Would you like to share any parting words for the fans?

MIÑOSO: Thank you forever. I recognize that the success of any character or any show is always driven by the people who watch it. I so appreciate you all giving me the opportunity to live in this guy’s skin for so many years. It doesn’t go unnoticed by me how important your support for the character has been, and I’m forever grateful to all of you for letting me enjoy this for as long as I have.

I Was A Mess Watching Cruz Leave 51 In Chicago Fire’s 300th Episode. There Were ‘A Lot Of Tears’ On Set, Too

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

I Didn’t See Sophia Bush Comparing Her Time On Chicago P.D. To Megyn Kelly's At Fox News Coming

NCIS: Origins Cast Reacted To That ‘Terrifyingly Sad’ Revelation, And I’m Right There With Them

I Was Already In My Feelings Over Ernie Johnson’s Inside The NBA Retirement. Then I Saw Tributes From Ben Stiller, Draymond Green And More

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

51 is officially down another firefighter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Warning! Spoilers for Season 15, Episode 5 of Chicago Fire, “Three Hundred,” lie ahead.

Chicago Fire has had a revolving door of cast members over the 14 years it has been on the air. And unfortunately, that door is still spinning amid Season 15 on the 2026 TV schedule. Fans have already had to say goodbye to Dermot Mulroney’s Dom Pascal, but and that's not all. This week marked the NBC hit's 300th episode as well as the departure of Joe Cruz, played by OG cast member Joe Miñoso. While I was a mess watching Cruz’s farewell to 51, there were apparently “a lot of tears” on set, too, as Miñoso told me.

Following Season 14's cliffhanger, the Season 15 premiere revealed that no one at 51 died. However, Cruz broke his spine as a result of a collapse at the warehouse, and despite him doing extensive rehab, it would basically take a miracle for him to be a firefighter again. Wednesday’s episode, “Three Hundred,” saw Cruz getting the news that if he continued to do rehab and work hard, he could go back to work in a matter of months.

That being said, following the birth of his and Chloe’s twin girls, Cruz decided to take a step back and go to a great rehab facility in Minnesota close to Chloe’s parents. He had a very vulnerable chat with Severide about his decision, and his goodbyes with Hermann, Mouch, Tony, and Capp were equally emotional and funny. I definitely couldn’t stop crying while watching it, especially when Cruz stopped by Otis’ memorial and talked with his late friend. Unsurprisingly, Miñoso got emotional while filming his character's sendoff, and he spoke to CinemaBlend about the final scene he shot:

A lot of tears. The last scene that we did was with me and Taylor [Kinney], and it was where I'm explaining why I'm leaving. And so much of it felt really, kind of like true-to-life. It felt like a lot of things that Joe and Taylor would have said to each other, and it was such a, I think, you know, probably the most human and intimate scene that Taylor and I have ever had the opportunity to do.

Given Miñoso had been part of the cast since the very beginning, it's honestly got be hard to imagine Chicago Fire without him, and 51 without Cruz. It’s great, however, that he was able to have that opportunity with Taylor Kinney not only for their characters, who are basically brothers, but for themselves, too. That’s not the only reason it was emotional, though, as Miñoso will also have a core memory that lasts a lifetime:

It was, first and foremost, such a gift to be able to have that scene with him just because we've never really sat face-to-face as equals like that in a scene. It felt really special. The fact that it was the very last scene on the stage with my wife there, who I met on the show as a makeup artist 10 years ago, and we just celebrated our 10th anniversary. It'll be an implanted memory forever. It's like one of those core memories. It's definitely going to be one of the shining bright ones in my mind for a long time.

It was announced over the past summer that Miñoso would be leaving Chicago Fire, and many fans were not happy about it. That being said, the fact that Miñoso was able to be ushered out with five episodes to wrap up Cruz’s story makes it just a little bit more bearable.

Following the Season 14 cliffhanger, though, there was no telling what the result would be, and how it could figure into Cruz’s departure. Chicago Fire has killed off a handful of characters following season-ending cliffhangers. Luckily, when it came to how Cruz would bow out, there was only one way to handle it, per Miñoso:

There were a lot of discussions between the writers and producers and myself in terms of how we wanted to say goodbye to the character. But [showrunner] Victor [Teran] was pretty adamant from the very beginning that he wanted to be able to explore a different type of trauma on this show. We haven't really been able to play this storyline in terms of our firefighters getting so hurt that they can no longer be the person they think they are.

Cruz has been such a staple at 51, and his passion for the firehouse and his job trumped just about everything, aside from his family. With Miñoso leaving, it really only made sense for him to decide to leave 51 because of his family. And Miñoso loved the way it all came together:

But at some point, they overcome it and then ultimately make the decision to walk away on their own terms. So, it was really an honor, a joy, a privilege. I feel very lucky that they gave me the material and thought and believed in me to be able to bring it to life.

Character exits are always tough in the One Chicago franchise, especially when it’s someone who has been around since the start of a show. But since Cruz, Chloe, the boys, and now their baby twin girls are only a couple of states away, it’s possible that this won’t be the last we see of them. Nevertheless, I'll probably continue to be an absolute mess whenever I revisit this episode of the show.

If anything, all episodes of Chicago Fire are streaming with a Peacock subscription, so there is plenty of Cruz to still enjoy. As for new episodes, those air on Wednesdays at 9 p.m. ET on NBC.

Passionate writer. Obsessed with anything and everything entertainment, specifically movies and television. Can get easily attached to fictional characters.

The Top 26 Looks in 2026 (So Far), According to 50 of Hollywood’s Biggest Stylists

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Hollywood Reporter presents its inaugural data-driven stylists’ survey, in which tastemakers voted on their peers’ most impressive looks this year. What’s being served: fashion as art.

In the three decades since red carpets have become synonymous with Hollywood couture, celebrity stylists have outpaced fashion editors, models, moguls and, more recently, content creators as the true arbiters of the town’s taste. No one else has the number of media impressions that top Hollywood stylists do, with many of them pulling in millions of followers. Of course, Beyoncé, Rihanna and Taylor Swift have 500 million-plus Instagram fans each, but much of the engagement is driven by what their stylists are putting on their backs.

That’s why, as this year winds down, THR reached out to the many pros who’ve now achieved fame in their own right for dressing the most visible personages in the world. What did we learn? The newish Parisian design duo behind Matières Fécales — Hannah Rose Dalton and Steven Raj Bhaskaran — have captured stylists’ hearts the most, while Matthieu Blazy’s Chanel, Jonathan Anderson’s Dior and Anthony Vaccarello’s Saint Laurent also number among Hollywood’s favorite designers. Feathers still are flying on red carpet fits for all genders. The sheer look continues to show off, but cutouts and two-piece looks are taking over the body-part wars. Lace, lingerie and leather are now completely red carpet-worthy. Slinky close fits and strong tailoring are forever. And so, apparently, is fashion’s idolization of Zendaya. We could have easily included three or more of her looks, but to keep the list varied and fun, we chose only one top fit per actor or actress. The methodology behind the list included tabulating the top 10 looks chosen by 50-plus stylists, prioritizing by the number of votes each look received, and then, to break ties, bringing in rankings. The result: a realistic look at coming trends and which designers and designs rank the highest for Hollywood, by the world’s top tastemakers.

At the NYC premiere of The Odyssey on July 14, America’s favorite zillennial stunned in Matières Fécales’ “Ange” gown. Styled by Law Roach, the micro-hand-pleated chiffon creation sported white rooster feather wings designed to evoke Zendaya’s character in the movie: Greek goddess Athena. “What I loved most about the Matières angel look,” says stylist Ilaria Urbinati (her clients: Dwayne Johnson, Barry Keoghan) “is how cohesively ethereal it felt from head to toe. It didn’t look costume-y. She looked like a painting. Not many could pull it off.” Adds stylist Tara Swennen (Kristen Stewart, Ali Wong), “It literally took my breath away.”

At the Met Gala on May 4, Chamberlain grabbed the limelight in custom Mugler by Miguel Castro Freitas. Inspired by impressionist painters, the one-of-a-kind rainbow dress, styled by Jared Ellner, took 40 hours to paint and four days to dry. “It really embodied the ‘Fashion Is Art’ Met Ball theme without feeling like a costume,” says stylist Tracy Shapoff (Molly Parker, Bill Maher). “The gorgeous hand-painting texture on the dress felt as though they’d painted a one-of-a-kind piece directly onto her body. I really felt like I was looking at a piece of artwork.”

Styled by Wayman + Micah, last Oscar season’s breakout Infiniti radiated in custom Thom Browne at the May 4 Met Gala. Made of 1.5 million hand-embroidered sequins and over 600 shades of silk fringe, the gown was inspired by the Greek sculpture of Venus de Milo. “There’s something surreal about it,” says stylist Avigail Collins (part of a team with husband Damian; they work with Elizabeth Olsen and St. Vincent). “Like wearable art, almost sculptural, the way the sequins, beading, intricate embroidery, craftsmanship and embellishments create an abstract silhouette, pushing the boundaries of fashion.”

Also wearing custom Thom Browne and also styled by Wayman + Micah, another of last awards season’s standouts, Taylor, grabbed fashion mindshare at the Actor Awards on March 1. The surrealist molded gown in gray and off-white was meant to evoke an abstract painting of a naked woman’s body, with ribbons dripping down the back. The “naked” anatomical bodice was made from over 160 pattern pieces put together via intarsia and tonal shading to mimic carved musculature; the gray tulle mermaid skirt was embellished with over 400,000 sequins in gold and white gold.

In a ruby-beaded, liquid-looking Chanel confection, Kidman, styled by Jason Bolden, turned heads at the Met Gala on May 4. Chanel’s Matthieu Blazy created the dropped-waist silhouette, with feathers at the hips and cuffs, a long dramatic train and surprising long sleeves. “What I loved most about that gown,” offers stylist James Yardley (Connor Storrie, Hannah Waddingham), “was its simplicity. Immaculate fit, and the placement of the feathers had such an impact. The cherry on top: the spinel and lapis lazuli ring, also from Chanel.”

Wearing this Miu Miu crystal corseted gown at the Oct. 1 Sydney premiere of Sense and Sensibility, Edgar-Jones, as styled by Dani Michelle, perfectly referenced Jane Austen’s era, with delicate ribbons as shoulder straps. “This strikes all the right notes,” declares stylist Ariel Tunnell (Rachel Bloom, Stephanie Beatriz). “The color, the sheer elements, the tones against her skin — so flattering. Add in the historical dressing of corsets and lacing, and it comes together in the coolest and most gorgeous way.”

Modelling custom Saint Laurent by Anthony Vaccarello at the Sept. 27 MTV Video Music Awards and styled by Chris Horan, the music provocateur reminded that the midriff is an erogenous zone. Goth and glam at the same time, Charli embodied a modern Morticia Addams. “I can’t think of anyone who knows who they are better than Charli XCX,” stylist Michael Fisher tells THR (Ethan Hawke, Jon Hamm, Jake Gyllenhaal). “Every time I see her on a carpet, she’s perfectly herself and looks effortlessly cool.”

Styled by Jahleel Weaver, she wore a custom metallic architectural creation by Maison Margiela artistic director Glenn Martens for the May 4 Met Gala. Inspired by the medieval buildings of the Flanders region of Belgium, it was sewn from duchess silk, recycled metal threads and 115,000 crystal beads alongside antique jewels and chains. The label’s Paris atelier spent 1,380 hours of handwork on what Rihanna described as “looking like a pearl emerging from an oxidized oyster shell,” a reference to her home country of Barbados.

The Schiaparelli couture lace gown worn by the model multihyphenate and styled by Mimi Cuttrell for the Cannes Film Festival was an homage to Jane Birkin. It required 130 artisans to create. Stylist Molly Dickson (Sydney Sweeney, Kaia Gerber) offers: “Everything Daniel Roseberry does at Schiaparelli is incredible. This dress balances delicate proportions and contrast perfectly. The crochet is intricate; the V of the neck is the perfect line. And let’s not forget the back! It has a black ribbon corset lacing up to the neck, providing the tiniest bit of edge.”

Only badass Theron, as styled by Leslie Fremar, could sex up this suit from Dior men’s fall 2026 collection, which she wore to the April 22 NYC premiere of Apex. “This look stood out to me,” says stylist Lee Harris (Helen Mirren, Drew Barrymore), “because in this red carpet world in which so many try to go over the top for attention, this was razor sharp and clean, with just one powerful accessory. She wore the look — the look didn’t wear her.”

In Celine at a Good Morning America appearance in February promoting Love Story, Kelly wowed stylists and fashion luminaries of all genders. Styled by Warren Alfie Baker, the combo of jacket, rep tie and jeans hearkened back to his character, JFK Jr. “I love when well-worn denim makes its way into celeb dressing because it’s always unexpected,” says stylist Erica Cloud (Robert Downey Jr., Mia Goth). “The homage to JFK Jr. in the ’90s was a great intro to Paul’s fashion presence. The little Celine details — minimal loafers, squared-off tie — were icing on the cake.”

Swinton’s striking Chanel couture cocoon silhouette, complete with vertical pleating and a soft, body-conscious drape, was styled by Jerry Stafford for Cannes. Notes stylist Sarah Slutsky Tooley (Maya Hawke, Emma Watson, Elizabeth Olsen): “I’ve looked to Tilda as a style icon since I began in fashion. This look has unexpected proportions, matching tomato red with white kitten heels. It’s so thoughtful, though so easy. She looks so cool, completely refreshing and unexpected. Fashion is so natural to her DNA.”

At the Jan. 28 L.A. Wuthering Heights premiere, Elordi drew admiration as a very tall, masculine figure in a Chanel cropped jacket, all styled by Wendi and Nicole Ferreira. “This Chanel look masters understated luxury with a relaxed, modern twist,” notes stylist Chris Brown (David Beckham, Luke Evans, Leo Woodall). “The oversized blazer, soft knitwear and cream trousers create an elegant tonal palette, while the loose fit keeps things effortlessly modern. It’s a lesson in quiet confidence.”

In Erdem at the MTV Video Music Video Awards, Sivan sported tuxedo suiting with sharp shoulders and a stiff, upturned collar, but from the waist down was adorned with ostrich feathers and satin and tulle trim. Styled by Marc Forne, this look was adapted from an Erdem dress in the spring/summer 2027 collection that was inspired by 1920s American aerialist Barbette, who performed in drag. Erdem used that as a jumping-off point to play with masculine and feminine tropes, combining strict tailoring and dramatic stage costume.

In a custom pink, plush velvet double-breasted suit with flared pants by Louis Vuitton, Pharrell cut an appropriately high-fashion figure at the Feb. 1 Grammys. Styled by Matthew Henson, the Vuitton men’s creative director also made matching suits for Clipse — the hip-hop duo also known as Malice and Pusha T, who were named by Pharrell himself — for their Grammy-night reunion. Pharrell also donned two Jacob & Co. Georgian-style yellow and white diamond riviera necklaces and diamond stud earrings.

In an intricate Saint Laurent black-lace ballgown at the Met Gala, Hollywood’s perennial cool girl was in top form, as styled by Danielle Goldberg. “The silhouette creates a striking contrast between sleek sensuality and vintage drama,” comments stylist Brown. “It’s a look that feels mysterious, tactile and unapologetically individual, with Saint Laurent’s signature rock ’n’ roll attitude running throughout.” Kravitz’s Met Ball date was not her fiancé, Harry Styles, but one of her most-worn designers, Saint Laurent creative director Anthony Vaccarello.

In 36-year-old Turkish fashion designer Dilara Findikoglu at the Met Gala, Ortega played up her Goth character Wednesday Addams with a full-on peekaboo look, styled by Enrique Melendez. “I love that the chest is covered and the cutouts are on the sides of this gown,” says Dickson. “It’s like a reverse of the expected, creating an interesting moment on the carpet.”

Pairing a vintage John Galliano brocade feather-cuffed coat with a micro-mini and red tights for the Feb. 5 London premiere of Wuthering Heights, Robbie caught lightning. Styled by Andrew Mukamal, the head-to-toe look is a brilliant study in dissonance, pairing modern and Gothic. “What we loved about this look,” says stylist Avigail Collins, “was the contrast between the romance of the 1992 archival Galliano coat and the sexiness of the black mini, red stockings and that clashing pink ribbon. An unexpected combination that works beautifully.”

Styled by Nick Royal and Peri Rosenzweig of Hardstyle, the original little monster wore a black feathered gown with a train by Matières Fécales to the Grammys. “The cut sold me,” says stylist Nico Amarca (Atticus Ross, Chase Hudson). “Feathers go straight up the neck, lock around her jaw like armor, a hard look. Gaga’s biggest looks have always been about shock. This one was about control: one material, all black, no gimmick. Choosing a young, independent house like Matières Fécales over a legacy name made it even better.”

Donning a white Schiaparelli suit with broad-brimmed hat while marrying Callum Turner at a London civil wedding on May 31, the singer-songwriter struck a Lorenzo Posocco-styled iconic silhouette. THR artistic director Alison Edmond explains, “It was all the more stylish by the location being so low-key; I loved that for them. She owned that Bianca Jagger reference, but made it Dua: the suit, hat, gloves, pumps, the flowers — perfection. There’s so much reality (in the dictionary sense of the word) about this couple: real love, real bodies, real sexiness … no fake relationship here.”

Styled by Jamie Mizrahi, Pascal shone at the Academy Awards in a custom Chanel jacketless tuxedo shirt with a white floral brooch made of silk and feathers, as unconventional as an Oscar look can get. “A guide to how to be a stylish man in formal wear, without being garish,” says stylist Melendez (Jenna Ortega, Inde Navarrette). “He hit the perfect medium. The exquisite tuxedo shirt with flower brooch moment added subtle drama we love to see in a carpet look, and that we always appreciate in men’s styling. The accentuated waist with the cummerbund showcased the fine tailoring.”

Styled by Anastasia Walker and showcasing a cropped tux by Mexican designer Patricio Campillo, the country hip-hop star made quite an impression. “Shaboozey walked into the Oscars and turned black-tie into a full showman moment!” says stylist Chaise Dennis (Tramell Tillman, Edward Norton.) “It’s impeccably tailored, totally distinct, so much fun. He put on a show. I also love the support of a small brand like Campillo on such a big stage.”

The Jan. 11 Golden Globes was the first time J.Law went sheer. Styled by Ryan Hastings, the custom semi-transparent Givenchy gown was designed by Sarah Burton. “The look was both floral and sheer,” enthuses stylist Melendez, “which doesn’t sound too exciting until you actually see it! The perfectly placed appliqués made this look the perfect blend of sultry and sweet, and the addition of the silk embroidered shrug instantly made it even more captivating — an almost street-style element added in the most elevated way.”

The Khaite backless custom leather halter-neck gown at the Emmys, as worn by The Diplomat star Russell and styled by Frank Fleming, was one of a number of edgy appearances. Her accessories matched the toughened look: high black stilettos, Buccellati garnet and diamond gold jewelry, and a slicked back, low ballerina bun. “This is a perfect look,” notes Cloud. “Equal amounts of nonchalance and edgy sexiness.”

Moore was on the judging panel at the Cannes Film Festival, and her red carpet entrances encompassed 10 amazing looks styled by Brad Goreski, including this full-on ballgown by the provocative Paris duo of Matières Fécales. “A hot-pink Matières Fécales gown at Cannes, before most people knew the brand — perfect irreverence,” says stylist Britt Mccamey (Tracee Ellis Ross, Sadie Sink). “I respect how much Demi and Brad care about art in fashion. No algorithm could have picked this for her, that was the whole point. It’s fun to have fun!”

“I enjoyed the detailing of this look,” says stylist Chloe Takayanagi (Sterling K. Brown, Kumail Nanjiani) of the details on Jordan’s custom-tailored Louis Vuitton Nehru-collar suit, styled by Jason Bolden for the Oscars. “I loved the brooch on the back of the neck and the watch chains, which seemed like a light nod to his character in Sinners. The mandarin, stand collar jacket was also a refreshing take on a classic-cut suit.”

Styled by Karla Welch, Reinsve wore a number of Louis Vuitton dresses throughout awards season, all of them stark and revealing at the same time. The one she wore to the Oscars was a red strapless column gown with a sweeping train. “This was one of my favorite looks of Renate’s, the simplest dress, so effortless,” describes stylist Jessica Paster (Emily Blunt, Quinta Brunson). “It’s a beautiful dress — the sleekness of the slit up the leg — and the color. Sooo chic.”

“I adored seeing Malkovich walk down the carpet in this look in Venice,” says THR’s Edmond of the actor’s Dior suit at the film festival. “He has a touch of the genius in his work, and stylist Jeanne Yang is certainly talented enough to know how to showcase the theatrical via the sartorial. The beautifully oversized suit with the tied and pleated collar: What a perfect mix of fashion and film, putting Jonathan Anderson’s flourishes on this incredible actor.”

The corsetry and exaggerated shape of Pugh’s Vivienne Westwood at the Sept. 30 NYC premiere of East of Eden strongly references her character’s early 20th century wardrobe. “This is the power of a strong silhouette,” observes Swennen of stylist Rebecca Corbin-Murray’s work. “The volume’s deliberate, yet does not overwhelm her. The fabric is so regal and opulent and the color — lime green/chartreuse — that’s something you don’t see often on the red carpet. I love it when people take risks, when they’re bold. It’s a very regal, luxurious look.”
