Dark Florals Are Giving Thanksgiving Outfits an Unexpectedly Romantic

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This Thanksgiving, we're swapping autumnal browns and oranges for deep plums, rich burgundy and dark navy florals.

Thanksgiving Day outfits probably conjure up images of chunky brown and burnt orange knits, suede knee-high boots, and corduroy mini skirts.

But this year, Thanksgiving is taking an unexpectedly romantic route. Rather than traditional autumnal hues and fabrics, seasonal styles are embracing dark florals this holiday season.

If you're unsure what to wear this Thanksgiving Day, dark floral maxi dress, blouses, and bottoms could be the twist your yearly outfit needs, even if all you're going to do is sit in the living room with your cousins.

With a gothic, almost witchy appeal, dark floral dresses, tops, and even pants offer a moodier alternative to harvest-inspired hues.

Instead of the bright colorways you'd see in spring and summer, winter and fall florals are defined by dark plums, rich burgundy, deep navy blues, often paired with '90s-inspired velvet overlays or warm corduroy.

Embrace a romantic gothic flair this Thanksgiving with fall's trending dark florals.

With its velvet stretch knit design, you can pair this plum-colored bodysuit with your favorite pair of jeans and some suede booties for an easy Thanksgiving Day look. A cowl neck style open back shows off just the right amount of skin.

Even pants are getting a dark floral update. Case in point, these wide-leg corduroys, which feature a high-waist fit and a muted red and sepia floral pattern.

A breezy gauze fabric is offset by a deep olive and burgundy micro floral print in this button-down shirt. Roll up the subtle puff sleeves for an effortlessly chic finish.

With its velvet floral pattern and built-in sash details, this body-skimming dress with a peek-a-boo cutout is giving major '90s vibes.

This boxy corduroy jacket with an allover moody floral print calls for your favorite black jeans and matching pointed mules. Gold floral buttons make it feel more expensive than it is.

More of a boho chic girl? This flowy maxi dress with a smocked bodice is made from a cotton poplin blend and features an intricate floral print. Pair it with riding boots for the ultimate fall look.

A bestseller, this quilted jacket is designed with lightweight insulation for a cozy feel. It's reversible so you're basically getting two looks in one.

This high-rise maxi skirt, which has a velvet floral burnout design, pairs with any black top you already have in your collection.

From its tiered ruffle skirt to its cutout open back, this scoopneck gown is elegant enough to wear to your next fall wedding.

You'll be the best dressed wherever you wear this lush velvet crop top with a sultry lace-up open back.

Channel a romantic gothic vibe in this semi-sheer top, complete with bell sleeves and fluttery ruffle neckline.

How Joe Miñoso Exits ‘Chicago Fire’ As Cruz In Season 15

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

SPOILER ALERT: The following reveals major plot points for NBC‘s Chicago Fire‘s Season 15 Episode 5 titled “Three Hundred.”

It’s the end of an era on NBC’s Chicago Fire, as we say goodbye to Joe Miñoso, who has played Firefighter Josef “Joe” Cruz since Season 1. And what better way to send him off than on the show’s 300th episode titled “Three Hundred.” Check out Deadline’s interview with Miñoso, where he talks about saying goodbye after close to 15 years.

It never gets any easier saying goodbye to actors who leave the series, especially those who have been on the show since Day 1 like Miñoso. But it certainly helps when we see them get their happily ever after, which was the case for Joe, who’s been having a rough go at things since the Season 15 premiere when he suffered a spine injury. Through hardships, the character learns a lot about himself and what he really wants out of life. Watch on Deadline Related Stories News 'Worth' Detective Drama From Mike Diliberti, Jake Coburn & Jack Carr In Development At NBC Late Night TV 'SNL' Pays Tribute To Patrick Stewart With Clip From His 1994 Episode

The episode opens with Joe getting physical therapy, showing off how far he has advanced in re-learning how to walk. You can feel how dedicated he is to improving his health and getting back to life by how he focuses on breaking his own record of steps.

Later, Joe shows up at 51 and surprises his team with a visit. He reunites with Capp (Randy Flagler), Tony (Anthony Ferraris), and Severide (Taylor Kinney), whom he shares a medical update with. With a big smile on his face, Joe rises from his wheelchair before them and jokes, “My legs finally got the memo.”

He tells the guys that Dr. Archer (Steven Webber from Chicago Med) let him know about some specialized recovery centers that help first responders get back to their jobs. He’s hopeful that within a few months, he will be cleared to get back to life as he once knew it. Seconds later, the fire truck arrives at the station, and Mouch (Christian Stolte) yells out loud, “Joe Cruz!” The rest of the team excitedly celebrates the visit before Severide beckons him to his office.

Battalion Chief Tomlin, played by Doug Savant of Melrose Place and Desperate Housewives fame, invites himself into Severide’s office first to tell him that they need to begin their search for Joe’s replacement. Tomlin gives Severide one week to submit the name of a qualified candidate before headquarters makes the choice for him.

Joe heads home where his wife, Chloe Cruz (Kristen Gutoskie), is busy around the house trying to get things in order while heavily pregnant with twins. She expresses her stress and frustration about all of the responsibilities that have fallen on her since the accident and that she desperately needs help. During the discussion, Chloe starts going into labor and her water breaks. Joe was certain they still had time before the babies would make their grand appearance, as expected, four weeks later, but it soon becomes very real that they’re running out of time.

Joe and their two sons rush Chloe to Gaffney Chicago Medical Center, where she gives birth to two little girls, whom they name Sofia and Elena. Dr. Archer stops by to congratulate the Cruz family and welcome Joe to the #girldad club. Archer reveals he had a chat with his physical therapist, who said his progress was remarkable and that he would hopefully be ready to return to work. The disappointed reaction from Chloe and the kids was expected, but Joe is finally realizing he needs to think about what his family needs now the most.

Joe reconnects with Severide and lets him know, with tears in his eyes, that he’s decided to not come back to 51. He and the family will be moving to Minneapolis to be near Chloe’s mom, who is excited to help them out with the newborns, leaving Joe time to join a program in the Minnesota city. Severide is stunned to hear his decision; after 12 years of riding together, nothing will ever be the same.

The episode ends with Joe paying a visit to Otis’ (Yuriy Sardarov) memorial, which he’s kept an eye on since his death in the Season 8 premiere in 2019. With a promise that Capp and Tony will keep the marble well looked after, Joe says, “I’ll be with you always, brother.”

Severide shows up from behind to congratulate his friend with a cigar in hand, for the man of the hour. The final beats of the episode show Joe surrounded by the devoted and beloved group of men and women he has worked with for more than a decade for a final photo. Right smack in the middle, Joe smiles for the photo, knowing that his contributions to the team will never be forgotten.

NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 5, 2026

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you like shoes.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

Six Hollywood Consequences of Tuesday’s Democrat Blockbuster

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

From the federal tax credit to cable-news trolling to AI regulation, the world looks a lot different today than it did last week before the election.

The massive showing by Democrats in Tuesday’s midterm elections impacts everyone who lives in this country. But the entertainment industry has a particular role, given its charge to tell stories — to explain America to itself. And now there’s suddenly a whole new chapter to write about a country that has decided to radically reject the politics of many of the past ten years.But man, that sounds awfully high-minded. There are far more concrete examples. Here are six entertainment realms that could look a lot different after Tuesday’s domination.Bring on the film money?The federal film tax credit had a little bit of momentum with Republican lawmakers like Tim Scott, Brian Jack and Nathaniel Moran and Democrats such as Adam Schiff, Laura Friedman and Linda Sanchez introducing a bill last month to establish the 20 percent credit for certain labor costs. Where does Tuesday leave it? Well, the good news is all the lead champions in Congress have won re-election and will be back to work next session (though Scott may have to spend a few extra minutes in the morning wiping egg off his face for his “unequivocal” assertion that Republicans would maintain Senate control).The question is whether they can rally enough fellow lawmakers behind it. On paper, Republicans would want to join the cause, as it’s a win for them at a time when those won’t be easy to come by. Doubly so for President Donald Trump, who will be eager to crow about…something, and bringing some jobs back to the U.S. will allow for the chirp.The snag is that the tax credit reduces the U.S. tax base at a time of massive deficits and when many Democrats are promising a restoration of benefits — i.e., the kind of thing you need a deep tax base for. Supporters will argue this is tax revenue that wouldn’t have happened anyway if production was going overseas (sometimes but not always true) and that it will benefit workers (yes, will trickle down, but the actual rebate goes to producers, some of the bigger of whom won’t be seen as needing a handout). On paper this should still have a decent shot. But Congressional turnovers can mess with priorities. Put this in the unknown column.Texas artists saw it all alongNo one around the cable-news dial could quite believe that Texas had elected a Democratic senator for the first time in 38 years or governor for the first time in 32. Harry Enten was so in disbelief he almost got into a fight with his accent.We should have paid closer attention to creatives. They’ve been telling us how it’s possible — telling us of another Texas — all along. Natalie Maines of the (then-Dixie) Chicks famously and at the expense of her career told a London audience at the start of the Iraq War in 2003 that “we do not want this war, this violence. And we’re ashamed that the president of the United States is from Texas.”But it’s been creeping in plenty of other Texas artists’ work too — in the films of Richard Linklater and his anti-exploitation 2006 movie Fast Food Nation and the arched eyebrow-to-false-jingoism Last Flag Flying in 2017. Mike Judge, pushing back against George W. Bush-era compliance in 2006’s retroactive documentary Idiocracy. Or even just the work of an Austinite like David Modigliani and his 2019 nonfiction piece Running with Beto. (Actually, Beto walked so James Talarico could run.) We haven’t always paid attention.No doubt we’ll soon start hearing other artists around the country tell us a place is a lot more Democratic than its Republican politicians want to project. After Tuesday, it’s worth listening.Anti-media trollingYou know how you’re going for some minor medical procedure and the doctor’s like, “In the short term, it will hurt like hell, but then you’ll feel a lot better?”I suspect we’ll get a whole lash-out at mainstream media figures by Trump and members of his administration after Tuesday’s drubbing. “Outlets are failing,” “hosts should be fired,” you know the lyrics. But lame ducks tend to get lamer as their term wears on, and once Trump sees how little effect this is having — the meager few days of Jimmy Kimmel suspension he got after the Charlie Kirk joke last year will practically look like a Colbert canning by comparison — I suspect we’ll start seeing a lower-key approach or just his attention turned elsewhere.Spasms from the FCC are still possible, Brendan Carr writing letters to this media company or not asking them to drop by for a not-so-friendly visit. But how hard do you want to go when — oh yes, you’re not a presidential cabinet department but an agency overseen and funded by….Congress? The Carr witch hunts and saber rattles of the past two years are going to get a lot quieter. Who knows? Maybe the media companies will stop quaking in their boots and even restore a DEI program or two.David Ellison’s true colorsFor months we’ve been told by Ellison apologists — and David Ellison — that the new owner of Warner Bros. wasn’t actually a Trumper; he just played one at UFC fights. Ellison needed Trump to get his deals through, so it was all realpolitik. Well, now he doesn’t need that — in fact, if anything, a Trump association hurts him with a new Congress. So does he reveal himself to be the pragmatist his defenders say (or even the liberal on many issues that he says he is)? Or, with his Trump cover falling away, does he just unmask as the conservative he’s been up all along? First test up is Alex Gibney’s Musk documentary, coming in a few months from HBO. Does he still release it despite its targeting of the leading GOP donor? Musk just paid a lot of money to Skydance, potentially so HBO doesn’t air it, though he has bigger challenges to worry about right now. Related Stories Midterms 2026 Nithya Raman Projected to Win Los Angeles Mayor's Race, Defeating Karen Bass News Best Memes and TikToks After Midterms' Blue Wave, From Regina George to 'The Office' References

And there will be all sorts of other markers — films to buy, TV shows to air, news divisions to revamp — for one of the bigger moguls operating today. We’ll see what he’s really made of soon enough. At the very least John Oliver could rest a little easier today. Speaking of Musk, and getting things right, Alex Gibney’s idea that if we just pull back the curtain his weakness will be revealed was practically born out in lockstep on Tuesday as Musk’s half a billion went for naught. “The Great and Terrible Oz kept people in check because they were afraid of his visage that loomed over everybody, and he used that power to exert an enormous amount of control,” Gibney told THR last month. “But when a little dog pulled aside the curtain, and you can see a frumpy man operating gears and levers, suddenly he wasn’t so terrible and he wasn’t so great.” Exactly.One loser in all of this: Rob Bonta and the AGs who garnered a…modest settlement from Ellison in his WBD acquisition as pressure mounted from various politicians and leaders. Had the attorneys general just held out six more weeks, they would have had all of Congress and a whole new vibe on their side.

Celebrity…non-activism?The Hollywood model for this electoral season has been really interesting. Many people you talk to around the entertainment business and beyond have said some version of Hollywood sitting this out. But that’s not exactly true. Entertainers lent plenty of financial muscle to these midterms — see under: all the fundraisers thrown by A-listers, from Damon Lindelof for James Talarico to Shonda Rhimes for Jon Ossoff, along with all the donations from the likes of Chuck Lorre, Sally Field and Casey Bloys. Damn, Don Henley alone gave a half-million dollars to various candidates in Texas.

What happened a lot less were public campaign appearances. Celebrities did that nonstop in 2024, and it either didn’t help or got them (and their Democratic candidates) pulverized. If your party’s message is “return power to the people,” then some very rich red-carpet types standing there telling you how to do it sort of jams the signal and generally casts an elitist cloud over your message. We ain’t in 2004 anymore.

No doubt 2028 will bring plenty of big names stumping for candidates. People want to help, and famous people want to help famously; nothing wrong with that. But after the stark contrast between 2024 and now, we might see a different tack. Maybe a candidate doesn’t turn down Beyoncé who wants to show up at a campaign event. But maybe also Beyoncé thinks twice before offering? It’s not like stumping makes celebs’ lives easier either.

One other change: the idea of celebs running for office. A time-honored tradition. But Michele Tafoya just rode her Sunday Night Football popularity to the Republican nomination for Senate in Minnesota — then got trounced by 14 points. I mean, if a celebrity can’t get elected in Minnesota…That said, if McConaughey scratched his political itch and tossed his cowboy hat in the ring for, say, Ted Cruz’s seat in four years, how many of us would object?AI pullbackWhat kind of actual regulation we’ll get on AI remains to be seen. Ed Markey may have promised all those subpoenas for tech executives, and there’s a general air of bring-Big-Tech-in, but actually stopping Silicon Valley from developing new models and products is hard. It would be like trying to stop politicians from issuing press releases — it’s what they do. Also, there’s a pacing misalignment because Congress moves slowly and writes laws and tech companies move fast and write code. Also, tech companies have a lot of money and a lot of lobbyists.All those caveats said, the winds blow differently now. Many of next term’s 259 House Democrats and 54-56 Senate Democrats got there by promising tougher oversight on AI, particularly on data centers, and they’ll be incentivized to make some moves. And even if Trump won’t sign bills of that kind, the numbers are now lopsided enough that Senate Democrats would just need to peel off a few Republicans to get a 60-vote filibuster. (Josh Hawley, John Curtis and John Kennedy of Missouri/Utah/Louisiana come to mind; they’ve all expressed Big Tech-skeptical feelings.)All of this could slow down how quickly the models get developed and thus how fast they get adopted by studios or consumers. Good news for creatives, most of whom are fine with AI as a limited-use tool, less so as a soul- and job-destroying blunt instrument of automation.

Photo Credit: Taylor Swift The Eras Tour Wikimedia Commons / Paolo Villanueva

The concert might be the reason to travel, but Trip.com Group wants to make sure it's not the only thing fans spend money on. Tickets and hotel rooms are just the opening act.

Trip.com Group wants to turn concert trips into longer and more lucrative travel bookings. It is eyeing a move beyond tickets and hotel stays to offer fans everything from local attractions to ground transportation and dining experiences.

The company is betting that fans traveling to see their favorite artists are an opportunity to capture more of their overall travel spending, and particularly as major concerts often draw audiences across borders.

“Most concertgoers ideally wanted to spend an average of four days at the destination,” said Edison Chen, vice president of destination marketing and strategic alliances at Trip.com Group, at a media briefing in Singapore on Wednesday.

A fan flying in for a show can likely also be persuaded to spend a few extra days exploring the destination, said Chen. That creates opportunities for the company to sell attractions, transport and other experiences.

The World's 50 Best Restaurants, According to ‘The 50’ Best List

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Asador Etxebarri from the Spanish Basque Country won the title of world's best restaurant Wednesday night when The 50, the organization formerly known as 50 Best, announced its annual ranking during a ceremony in Lima, Peru. It was the first such event for The 50 in South America.

Situated between Bilbao and San Sebastián, Etxebarri claims some of the world's most famous chefs as major fans of Bittor Arginzoniz, who matches the characteristics of the wood in the grill with the plants and proteins sizzling over the flames. It was No. 2 on The 50 in 2025 and maintains one Michelin star. Tasting menus are around $337.

Wednesday was also a big night for Atomix in New York, which came in at No. 11 and was named Best Restaurant in North America. Junghyun Park took home the Chef's Choice Award.

A spot on The 50 is among the highest honors a restaurant can receive. (Disclosure: Bon Appétit editor in chief Jamila Robinson is a 50 Best Academy Chair.) The list favors some of the most expensive, elaborate cooking in the fine-dining world. It often rewards tasting menus that employ molecular gastronomy, but also celebrates the simple elegance of a Spanish grill, the sourcing mission of an Argentine steakhouse, or the laidback cool of Parisian neo-bistro.

This year’s ceremony ran into controversy earlier in the year when it was announced that it would be hosted in Abu Dhabi, which has faced criticism for human rights violations. In June, it was announced that the ceremony would instead be hosted in Peru, home to Maido, the Lima restaurant that won No. 1 last year. Maido is not eligible for this year’s list. After winning the top spot, it moves to the organization’s Best of the Best list alongside other past number ones like Barcelona’s Disfrutar and New York’s Eleven Madison Park.

Quintonil landed at No. 4. The two-Michelin starred restaurant in Mexico City led by Jorge Vallejo and Alejandra Flores, serves dishes that revolve around local, pre-Hispanic ingredients, which come together in energized and unexpected ways throughout its multi-course prix-fixe menu. A November menu listed on its website was priced around $327 per person for a meal that included lobster in a pipián verde with makrut lime, and an heirloom white corn tamal made with ayocote beans and young corn cream.

At Alchemist in Copenhagen (No. 3), chef Rasmus Munk oversees a meal that skews sci-fi with dishes that mimic brains and eyeballs and a show that advertises “up to 50 impressions,” both edible and “purely experiential.” It holds two Michelin stars and was ranked fifth on The 50 last year.

Gaggan in Bangkok (No. 2) claimed the title of Asia's Best Restaurant behind the creative, genre-bending Indian cooking of Gaggan Anand.

Critics of the World’s 50 Best have raised questions around its voting practices, which do not demand that voters pay for their own meals. The organization, for its part, explains that the list is voted on by more than 1,100 voters across 28 regions, a quarter of whom are new each year. The voting body is made up of equal parts chefs and restaurateurs, food writers, and “well-travelled gourmets,” as the The 50 website puts it. Aside from the Academy Chairs, voters must remain anonymous.

There is no set criteria for what makes a restaurant the best, and “every style of restaurant in the world is eligible,” though the list skews completely towards high-end dining even as other best-of links have begun to include street food venues, for instance.

Ana Ros, the chef at three Michelin-starred Hisa Franko in Slovenia, published a statement on Instagram last month explaining why she would not attend the ceremony in Lima. “Success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system,” she wrote. “…When visibility becomes the currency, the ability to promote a restaurant in this system risks becoming as important as the work happening inside it.”

Ros, who won the award for best female chef from The 50 in 2017, told Bon Appétit that restaurants and tourism boards “more or less” know who the anonymous voters are, and they often have to make decisions about whether to pay for travel, meals, and other entertainment to try and boost their standing.

“With more rules and a little bit more control, this list can really shine again,” she said in an interview.

Hisa Franko was No. 60 on the ranking of 51-100 this year. It was 69th last year and No. 48 in 2024.

Ros said seeing her restaurant at any place on the list was “a fantastic result” and she would be wishing everyone all the best at the ceremony.

39. Schloss Schauenstein, Fürstenau, Switzerland

38. Atelier Moessmer Norbert Niederkofler, Brunico, Italy

34. La Coquillage, Saint-Méloir-des-Ondes, France

28. Trèsind Studio, Dubai, United Arab Emirates

These are the 19 Best New Restaurants of 2026.

In Mexico City, gorditas are a blank canvas for creative fillings such as stringy quesillo, spiced chorizo, or even a whole fried egg.

Harold Greene Dies: Longtime L.A. News Anchor Was 82

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Harold Greene, the likeable longtime news anchor for many of L.A.’s local news stations, died last night. His passing was announced by family. Greene was 82.

At various junctures in his career, Greene worked for ABC7 Eyewitness News, KCAL 9 News and CBS 2 News. Greene also worked in San Diego for a period, and legend has long had it that his on-air look at the time (including an impressive mustache) inspired elements of the Ron Burgundy character in Anchorman. Will Ferrell has denied this, however.

Greene got his first job in L.A. as a reporter and producer for KABC. Shortly thereafter, he left for the San Diego affiliate, KCST-TV (now KNSD). He then jumped to the NBC affiliate at the time, KGTV, to co-anchor the news.

In 1978, he co-anchored the news in L.A. with Eyewitness News legend Jerry Dunphy. He became ABC7’s weekend anchor with Joanne Ishimine in 1982 before taking over the anchor chair for the 4 p.m. and 6 p.m. weeknight editions of Eyewitness News after Dunphy left. He later moved to the 5 p.m. and 11 p.m. slots. At ABC, Greene also hosted locally produced light-news programs such as Hollywood Close-Up and A.M. Los Angeles.

In 2001, he moved to CBS 2 and was part of a formidable on-air lineup that included L.A. TV News stalwarts Ann Martin and sportscaster Jim Hill. Later, Laura Diaz and weatherman Johnny Mountain joined the crew, reuniting members of KABC’s Eyewitness News team from the 1980s and early 1990s. Greene and Martin were laid off by KCBS/KCAL in 2008.

Greene received 10 Golden Mike awards, the Columbia Graduate School of Journalism Award, two Emmys, three Edward R. Murrow Awards and twelve L.A. Press Club Awards.

His daughter Courtney announced the news, writing that her father had “signed off for the last time” on Tuesday. She added that she and her mother were with Greene when he passed.

‘We have a feeling he’s already settling into the great newsroom in the sky, no doubt with a few suggestions on how things should be run.”

A memorial is being planned for after the holidays.

Andrew Garfield Says He ‘Never Had Any Interaction’ With Sam Altman While Filming ‘Artificial,’ Despite Altman Claiming They Waved to Each Other: ‘I Was Very Confused By That’

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Andrew Garfield might be playing OpenAI CEO Sam Altman in the forthcoming film “Artificial,” but the actor is maintaining that he’s never interacted with the tech billionaire — despite Altman’s claim that the two waved to one another while the film was shooting outside his home.

Garfield was asked about Altman’s anecdote in a recent GQ interview, and he said he “wasn’t there.” “No, I saw that interview and I replayed it a few times to try and discern what he was actually saying, because I was very confused by that. I never had any interaction with him,” said Garfield. “I’ve never seen him in real life. We were shooting outside of his house. It was an eerie day, for sure. We were told that he wasn’t home and his security people were there. I think I saw one security guard. But, no, I never saw Sam, so he maybe mistook someone else for me. I have no idea, but I’ve never waved at Sam. I’ve never seen him. I was curious about that.” Related Stories Benjamín Vicuña, Star of Netflix Hit Series 'Envious,' Joins ‘My Last Stand' by Goya-Winner Matías Bize

He also added that he “didn’t have a body double,” so that couldn’t have caused the confusion, though a representative for OpenAI told the magazine that “Altman was home during the filming and reiterated that he waved at Garfield.”

Altman had previously told Vanity Fair he ran into the production filming near his house.

“There were these notices on our street that there was going to be some filming or whatever. I didn’t realize what was going on. I paid no attention,” Altman said. “And then one morning, I was walking down the street to get coffee and there were all these cameras and there was a guy dressed exactly like me with my same backpack walking so much that I felt I was in some weird glitch of the simulation or something. It was very strange. And then I immediately figured out what was happening.”

He added, “We waved and went on, but it was really weird. I mean, it’s like a strange thing to see someone that has nailed your kind of quirky mannerisms or whatever…I waved at him that one time and that was it.”

Directed by Luca Guadagnino, “Artificial” opens in theaters on Christmas Day from Neon. Set during the tumultuous time when Altman was fired and then rehired by the AI giant in 2023, the film also stars Ike Barinholtz as co-founder Elon Musk, Monica Barbaro as former OpenAI CTO Mira Murati, “Anora” breakout Yura Borisov as former OpenAI chief scientist Ilya Sutskever and Mark Rylance as Nobel Prize winner Geoffrey Hinton.

Predicting Thomas Tuchel's England squad for Croatia, Spain UEFA Nations League fixtures

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

By Ben Sully | 05 Nov 2026 02:38, Last updated: 05 Nov 2026 03:32

England will complete the 2026-27 Nations League league phase with fixtures against Croatia and Spain this month.

The Three Lions will welcome Croatia to Wembley on November 12 before they head to Atletico Madrid's Metropolitano for a test against the world champions on November 15.

Thomas Tuchel's side head into the final two fixtures in second place in Group A3 after taking nine points from their first four matches since finishing in third position at the 2026 World Cup.

After starting with a narrow 3-2 defeat against Spain at Wembley, England recorded three consecutive victories by a combined 12-0 scoreline to all but seal a quarter-final berth.

The Three Lions will look to mathematically secure a last-eight spot and challenge Spain for the honour of clinching first place.

Ahead of Tuchel's squad announcement on Friday, November 6, Sports Mole predicts which players will make the cut for the final two matches of the Nations League league phase.

There appears to be little uncertainty surrounding the first two names on what is expected to be a three-man list of goalkeepers.

Everton’s Jordan Pickford and Leeds United’s James Trafford are guaranteed to receive the call-up from Tuchel, with the real focus on whether Tuchel will pick a number one for this international window or share the game time like he did in the opening four Nations League matches.

Pickford has long been seen as England’s number one, but he had to watch the opening two games against Spain and the Czech Republic from the bench as Tuchel offered Trafford the chance to make his case to be his first-choice goalkeeper.

Trafford then made way for Pickford to feature in the 7-0 away win over Croatia and the 3-0 home victory against the Czech Republic.

While his position may not be as certain as it once was, Pickford continues to close in on a century of England caps, with the 32-year-old just seven appearances away from reaching that impressive milestone.

In regard to the third-choice goalkeeper, Dean Henderson is set to get the nod over Brighton & Hove Albion’s Jason Steele after recently making his return to action from an ankle injury.

Predicted England goalkeepers for upcoming Nations League fixtures: Dean Henderson, Jordan Pickford, James Trafford

Tuchel initially picked nine defenders for the last international window before Newcastle United’s Tino Livramento pulled out due to injury.

Livramento remains unavailable for selection, along with England’s first-choice right-back Reece James, who is expected to be out until the new year after undergoing knee surgery.

In more positive news, Djed Spence, who was one of the standout players of England’s World Cup campaign, is back in contention after making six appearances for Inter Milan since returning from injury.

Trent Alexander-Arnold, meanwhile, has struggled for game time in recent weeks due to competition from Federico Valverde and Denzel Dumfries.

However, he could still get the nod for England after impressing in his two appearances in the last international window.

Jarell Quansah can also cover at right-back as well as offering an option in central defence, while Lewis Hall and Nico O’Reilly are likely to be the two left-backs in the squad.

Ezri Konsa, Marc Guehi and Jarrad Branthwaite are widely expected to be among Tuchel’s central defensive options, while injuries to John Stones and Trevoh Chalobah could open the door for Levi Colwill, who has started eight of Chelsea’s nine Premier League games this season.

Dan Burn is another name who could be in the mix, but while he played a useful role at the World Cup, Tuchel may be reluctant to bring the 34-year-old back into the fold as he keeps one eye on his plans for the European Championship in 2028.

Predicted England defenders for upcoming Nations League fixtures: Trent Alexander-Arnold, Jarrad Branthwaite, Levi Colwill, Marc Guehi, Lewis Hall, Ezri Konsa, Nico O’Reilly, Jarell Quansah, Djed Spence

The main headline news for England's midfield options is the imminent return of Declan Rice, who missed the last international window due to load management.

Rice is expected to form a central midfield pairing with Elliot Anderson, a player who continues to grow week by week under Enzo Maresca at Manchester City.

Myles Lewis-Skelly started three of the first four Nations League fixtures in Rice's absence, suggesting that Tuchel sees him more as a central midfielder than a left-back.

The Arsenal man should make the cut again, although he may have to settle for a substitute role this time around.

Alex Scott is someone who is almost definitely set to miss out on selection despite impressing in his 70-minute display against Croatia last month.

The Bournemouth star picked up a thigh injury in the emphatic win and has since missed seven games for the Cherries.

While he is close to a return to action, the November international window will come too soon, which may open the door for Jordan Henderson to make the squad for the first time since suffering a wrist injury at the World Cup.

James Garner was called up as a replacement for Kobbie Mainoo in September, and he could retain his spot ahead of the Manchester United midfielder, who has only recently returned from injury.

Looking at attacking midfielders, Jude Bellingham will be one of the first names on Tuchel's list, and he is set to join the national team in a positive mood after scoring in Real Madrid's last two matches.

Morgan Gibbs-White was a late call-up for the last squad, but is likely to miss out this time due to his underwhelming form in a struggling Nottingham Forest side.

There is a possibility that Tuchel may look to the future with Fulham star Josh King, who is an outside contender for a call-up after firmly establishing himself as a key player at Fulham this term.

Predicted England midfielders for upcoming Nations League fixtures: Elliot Anderson, Jude Bellingham, James Garner, Jordan Henderson, Myles Lewis-Skelly, Declan Rice

The front three of Bukayo Saka, Anthony Gordon and Harry Kane scored 11 goals between them across the first four UEFA Nations League fixtures as the trio linked up to clinical effect.

Unfortunately, it is a forward line that is guaranteed to be broken following the news that Saka is set for a spell on the sidelines with a thigh injury.

Noni Madueke has stepped up for Arsenal in Saka’s absence and is likely to be asked to do the same for England’s final two league phase matches.

Madueke’s Arsenal teammate Eberechi Eze is someone who appears to be trusted by Tuchel and should be on the list when the squad announcement is made on Friday, especially as another left-sided option, Marcus Rashford, is unavailable due to injury.

Liverpool’s exciting youngster Rio Ngumoha is also set to make the cut after making his first two competitive international appearances in the away and home games against the Czech Republic.

Meanwhile, Chelsea’s Morgan Rogers will be full of confidence when he joins the England squad after netting three goals in his previous five appearances.

Rogers appears to be stealing the limelight from his friend and teammate Cole Palmer, who appeared to frustrate Tuchel when he pulled out of his most recent squad with a minor issue.

The England boss may be tempted to offer Palmer another chance, especially with Saka sidelined, but it is clear that he has doubts over the strength of the 24-year-old’s desire to play for his country.

In regard to the centre-forward spot, England captain Harry Kane is a guaranteed starter and, barring injury, he will break his country’s all-time appearance record against Croatia at Wembley.

The biggest question is who will provide cover for the 2026 Ballon d’Or winner, with England struggling to find someone who can get anywhere near the level Kane produces.

Calvert-Lewin is not operating at the same elite level as Kane, but he has scored four goals in nine Premier League appearances this season and is likely to be the backup striker for the upcoming international window.

Tuchel is expected to only pick two strikers, but if he wants to add a third to his squad, he could look to the Championship where Middlesbrough’s Will Lankshear is the leading scorer with 13 goals to his name.

The in-form striker has been a regular for the England Under-21s, but with qualifying for next summer’s Under-21 European Championship all wrapped up, it could be a good opportunity to offer him a taste of life in the senior setup.

Predicted England attackers for upcoming Nations League fixtures: Dominic Calvert-Lewin, Eberechi Eze, Anthony Gordon, Harry Kane, Noni Madueke, Rio Ngumoha, Cole Palmer, Morgan Rogers

Stevie Wonder’s Eviction Lawsuit Escalates as Rent-Free Resident Demands Repairs to Walls and Plumbing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Stevie Wonder‘s reported one-time fiancée is pushing back against his attempts to boot her from the Hollywood Hills home she’s been living in rent-free since 1988.

Carmen Efferson filed a response to Wonder after his legal team sued her last month in a Los Angeles superior court and demanded she move out of Wonder’s three-bedroom home. In the Tuesday filing obtained by Rolling Stone, Efferson is accusing Wonder of breach of warranty of habitability — claiming he failed to maintain the property in conditions fit for human living. Among the complaints made in the filing, Efferson has alleged the home has damp or leaking ceiling/walls; holes in the walls/floor/carpet; damaged window screens and plumbing; dampness/mold, and more.

Efferson has asked a judge to order Wonder to pay for her attorney fees, make repairs to the home, and reduce the monthly rent to a “reasonable rental value” until the conditions are corrected. In the filing, Efferson also claims that she “has paid rent and/or provided other nonmonetary consideration or services in exchange for the right to occupy the premises.” Elsewhere, she disputes the title of the property and claims Wonder is “not the owner of the subject premises and therefore lacks standing” to evict her.

A lawyer for Wonder did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Wednesday.

In Wonder’s complaint filed Oct. 22, Efferson and the singer allegedly reached an oral agreement allowing her to live their rent-free for nearly 40 years. However, when it came time to access the house as part of Wonder’s property insurance renewal, representatives for the singer attempted to enter the home three times in July for inspection. Efferson refused to allow them to enter the property, and instead shot an email to Wonder’s lawyer David Huff saying “fuck the fuck off,” according to exhibits in the lawsuit obtained by Rolling Stone. Now He’s Rebuilding His Life — And Gunning for a Comeback

“Regarding your ORDERS of entering into the place where I am living, I do not consent. Do not make any attempt to make any kind of contact with me,” Efferson wrote to Huff in response to certified mail requesting entry to the property. After a few more emails, another lawyer informed Efferson that the oral agreement between her and Wonder had been revoked due to noncompliance and attorneys were serving her an eviction notice with a move-out date of Aug. 27, 2026.

That deadline came and went without Efferson departing the property, resulting in the civil lawsuit against her. Wonder is asking a judge to order Efferson be removed from the Hollywood house, as well as pay damages of $600, the amount he now owes due to lapsed property insurance renewal.

Blue Wave Sweep: What Does Democratic Shift Mean for Hollywood and the Media?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Democratic rout in the midterms was a referendum on President Trump, prices and the war in Iran. But it will have impacts that go well beyond the determinative issues — touching topics of key interest to Hollywood studios, broadcasters and their teams of government relations professionals.

Here’s a quick rundown of some of the key open questions as Washington adjusts to a new, blue reality for the final two years of Trump. Related Stories Benjamín Vicuña, Star of Netflix Hit Series 'Envious,' Joins ‘My Last Stand' by Goya-Winner Matías Bize

Hollywood’s top legislative priority is to pass a federal tax incentive by the end of the year. The bill is bipartisan — uniting Trump and Adam Schiff — so in theory, a looming change of party control shouldn’t affect it.

But any disruption — and the election was a massive disruption — probably doesn’t help build momentum. It also doesn’t help that Trump seems to be wavering, telling a rally last Friday that people in Hollywood are “all calling me kissing my ass” for tax credits. “I’ll probably do it. Should I do it?” he asked the crowd, which booed. “We’ll have to think about it.”

The industry is putting stock in that “I’ll probably do it,” but it wouldn’t be the first time Trump had let someone down. Congressional Republicans, meanwhile, are in shock, and are turning their attention to recriminations and to a leadership race. They could still get Hollywood tax credits done, but it’s not top of mind. For their part, House Democrats may want to hold off on any bipartisan deals until the next Congress, when they will hold all the leverage.

Democrats are more supportive of Hollywood than Republicans are, so the odds may improve in the 120th Congress. But that would require starting over. Out of the 31 Republican cosponsors in the House and Senate, 10 will not be back next year. The bill also benefits from the idea of a crisis requiring immediate action — which could be hard to sustain over a longer legislative process. “The iron is very hot right now,” Schiff said in September. “We’re all very focused on this. The president is focused on this. I wouldn’t want to see that bleed into next year.”

Democratic control of Congress won’t flip the Federal Communications Commission. But it will lead to greater oversight of chairman Brendan Carr, who has spent the last two years jawboning broadcasters over Jimmy Kimmel’s jokes and launching inquiries into political commentary on “The View.” Democrats in Congress see Carr as a manifestation of Trump’s impulse to squelch opposing views, and are likely to use subpoena power to investigate past and future controversies. Whether that adds light or just heat is very much TBD.

The Corporation for Public Broadcasting went out of business last year when Congress stripped $1.1 billion in funding. That in turn threatened the budgets of hundreds of local stations, some of which have been forced to consolidate, and eliminated a significant source of funding for PBS and NPR.

With Democrats in charge, there’s at least the possibility of restoring the funding, though it would require a sidequest into the culture wars, as conservatives see NPR and PBS as vehicles of leftwing bias and indoctrination.

Supporters want more than just to recreate the CPB, which was built for the media environment of 1967. They hope to reinvent federal support for public media in a way that goes beyond broadcasting.

“We urge the incoming Congress to renew federal investment in public and community media after the loss of the Corporation for Public Broadcasting and build a system around what communities need today,” said Rima Dael, CEO of the National Federation of Community Broadcasters, in a statement. “Universal access to fact-based news, trusted local information, and cultural programming means reaching people on air and online, in the languages they use, at a time when reliable internet access remains uneven and AI can amplify misinformation and disinformation.”

The House Oversight Committee will have a full plate of corruption investigations to pursue. It may or may not want to use its time to dive into the media capitulations of 2025 — the “Melania” documentary deal and the payouts over Trump’s “60 Minutes” lawsuit, his George Stephanopoulos lawsuit, his deplatforming lawsuits against Twitter, YouTube and Meta. Committee members may also take a hard look at the Ellison family’s efforts to win Trump’s blessing for the Paramount-Skydance and Paramount-Warner Bros. mergers. Any such efforts may lead to prolonged battles over executive privilege. But if Congress goes down that road and is successful, it could open up a trove of internal communications revealing how major media companies approached their dealings with the president at the peak of his powers.

French bank resumes transfers after $133 million attack but leaves crypto loss unconfirmed

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Arkéa says attackers used its own account through a provider's platform, while a separate $108.8 million USDC trail leaves the bank-to-crypto link unproven.

French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa said almost all operations had resumed on Nov. 4 after an attack enabled fraudulent crypto transactions from its own account, delaying some customer transfers.

In a later clarification, the bank said attackers reached it through a service provider's platform and sent money to other banks. Those transactions may then have enabled conversion into cryptocurrency through specialized platforms.

Arkéa did not confirm that conversion or connect its transfers to specific crypto wallets.

The bank denied that the attack entered through Fortuneo, its online banking subsidiary, and said it did not use customer accounts. It described the incident as contained and said customers' holdings were unaffected.

Arkéa declined to confirm whether €118 million ($133 million) had been stolen and validated only their order of magnitude. It said some funds had been blocked and further recovery efforts were underway, making a definitive financial amount premature.

The amount involved in fraudulent transactions is not necessarily the bank's final loss once blocked funds and recoveries are accounted for. Arkéa said the sums would not threaten its financial strength.

Its initial statement described precautionary transaction suspensions and a gradual restart after checks and stronger security measures. The later update said almost all operations had resumed during the morning.

Arkéa said it was working with judicial and police authorities to determine the attack's origin and method.

Separately, blockchain analytics firm Bitquery reported tracing $108.8 million in USDC withdrawals from wallets it labels as belonging to Kraken, Crypto.com and OKX to 15 addresses on Nov. 1 and 2. The withdrawals ran from 12:25 UTC on Nov. 1 to 09:09 UTC the next day, roughly 21 hours.

Mountain Lions, Wildfires, & Volcanoes: Washington Finally

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After our longest day on the PCT (46 miles!) we took a well-deserved rest at a spa hotel with Nina and Rebecca. We sleep in, soak in the hot tub until we’re pruney, and eat as much as we can. There’s no laundry onsite so we wash our clothes in the bathtub and hang them to dry outside. After our attempt at running I have shin splints. My back chafed under the unfamiliar day pack and my skin is tender. Other than those issues and the expected fatigue, we’re mostly unscathed from our monumentous effort.

We spend time in the hotel prepping for our last few weeks on trail. We mail home items we’d shipped up to Portland from further down the trail, and I switch my film camera out for my mirrorless one. Finally, we’re ready to get to it.

We head to Cascade Locks and see some hiker pals out in town. They’re planning to stick around for PCT Trail Days. We had debated staying, but it would be another several days before the event starts and we don’t want to stick around that long.

We bid farewell to Nina and Rebecca and walk across the Bridge of the Gods, officially entering our last state of the PCT. The gorge is beautiful and windy and crossing the Columbia River via foot is a new experience.

We quickly enter the woods on the Washington side and begin to climb. The path is lined with blackberries and poison oak. One is good for snacking as we walk, the other we avoid. We’ve started to see many Southbound hikers at this point making their way into Cascade Locks for Trail Days. The trail quickly becomes hot and humid and we’re sweaty as we make our way uphill, climbing out of the gorge.

Now that we’re finally in Washington, it feels like the soap bubble has popped. The fires we’ve been hearing about are no longer far away. We have about 500 miles left of the PCT, something we’d accomplish in a few short weeks at our current pace if we could. But, we have to face the music – we can’t. The trail to the North of Snoqualmie Pass is closed up to Stevens Pass, and then the trail North of Rainy Pass to the Northern Terminus is closed as well. That’s about 100 miles of the remaining trail we cannot do. There are alternates, which we discuss ad nauseam. Should we go around the Snoqualmie closure and keep a continuous footpath? Should we end the hike at Rainy, and go to the border later? Should we break our footpath and just hike the available PCT miles? Or should we attempt an alternate? The SOBOs (southbound hikers) are bringing us news of poor air quality around Stevens Pass and Snoqualmie Pass and our lungs took a beating already in Oregon. I don’t want to hike alternates through Washington in poor air quality just to reach a border – that feels like it would be a pointless exercise in suffering. And on top of it all, we live in Washington. We are getting closer to home every day, and some of the alternates bring me less than an hour away from my house by car. I can’t imagine road walking in the smoke and being so close to home. It would be unenjoyable and not at all how I’d want to end my hike. At this point we’ve been hiking for almost 130 days. We have run out of things to talk about, so discussing what to do and how we’d feel about each option occupies most of our days.

We push on through Southern Washington. The forested trails can be humid and have more climbing that we’d seen in Oregon. We’re both feeling the effects of our huge day well into the next week. We are exhausted and end each day with less miles than planned. The scenery is nice though, and we get glimpses of the volcanos. One day we take an alternate to see a waterfall, and camp next to a group of popular lakes. With our shorter miles we arrive at camp earlier. Its nice to enjoy eating in the daylight and hanging around at camp. We even have time to swim.

My coworker Thom offered to bring us a re-supply outside of Trout Lake. This will keep us from having to take a day off trail, which will help keep our momentum going. He has also offered to bring us “freshies” and we send him a list of our current cravings. We hike to meet him at a trailhead and find a feast set up for us. He makes us meatball subs and has fresh fruit for us to devour. We sit in comfortable chairs for hours. While we skipped the popular town of Trout Lake, we’re happy with the quick detour. We’ve seen the town before and if we’d gotten off trail we fear we might not have gotten back on. This was also part of our reasoning for skipping Trail Days. Many of our friends who went decided to end their hike at Cascade Locks. The fires were too much to navigate, and people want to enjoy Washington. I don’t blame them.

We depart our personal re-supply stop with an extra special late birthday gift – fully cooked, shelf stable bacon. We mix it in with our pasta and have some for breakfast the next day. It hits the spot.

Thom’s resupply helped us with getting enough food to make it to White Pass. We head North, excited to pass by Mount Adams and through the Goat Rocks Wilderness. As we hike on, the first of the hikers getting back on trail from Trail Days start to filter in and we get to camp with thru hikers again. We get a bit of cell service and I immediately check Watch Duty – one of my most used apps right now. I see that there’s another fire, this one on the PCT. The trail between Steven’s Pass and Steheiken is closed now. Our hearts sink and its a sad evening. We now have only two options – take alternate routes for the entire last 250 miles of trail, or get off the trail at Snoqualmie Pass.

We head into the Mount Adams Wilderness with heavy hearts. The burn scars feel a bit more tragic than normal. We’ve been hiking apart more and more lately, just walking in our thoughts. I’m trailing Sarah by a few minutes, listening to my audiobook when I hear her say “STOP.” I stop dead in my tracks and look around. She’s ahead at the top of a small hill. I join her and she says she just saw a large mountain lion heading into the copse of trees to the left of where the trail wraps around a bend. We walk together, making noise and scanning the surroundings. We pass the trees and see a small cat hopping around playfully in the underbrush. “I thought you saw a big cat….” I say. “I did.” she responds.

The implication that the mother to this young mountain lion is somewhere nearby sinks in. We stand back to back and make our way down the trail. We don’t see another cat, but we hike within sight of each other for the rest of the trail.

The Mount Adams wilderness is gorgeous. We cross glacial streams, take in the massive mountain views, and see the sunset on the glaciers. It passes by so quickly, and before 2 days are over we’ve put the mountain behind us.

We enter the Goat Rocks Wilderness, a highly anticipated section for us. We’ve made plans to come here both when we’ve lived in Oregon and Washington and they’ve never panned out. The smoke forecast looks good and we’re excited for epic views. We push through another grueling, humid climb, exiting the forest to an amazing meadow. The trail continues to climb up to Cispus Pass. We marvel at the scenery. It feels like we’re in a different world. After crossing the pass we descend into another meadow. There are endless wildflowers and an icy stream flowing through our path. We look high up onto the rocks and see goats!! It feels like a fairytale. We set an early alarm for the next morning and watch the sunset reflect off of Mount Adams.

The next day we start hiking early and are glad of it. There’s an incredible inversion and the smoke layer has settled enough to give us good views.

We climb up towards Old Snowy mountain, pausing every few steps to turn around and take in Mount Adams. We can see Mount Saint Helen’s as well, and tons of wildflowers. We reach continue to climb and reach a turnoff to Old Snowy. We aren’t going to summit the peak, but we are going to take the alternate that brings us up closer to the summit and skips a sketchy, icy snow traverse. There’s a small bit of snow to navigate on the alternate but it isn’t nearly as steep as the one we’re skipping. Then we’re on shale, getting epic views of Mount Rainier. Sadly, we can see the smoke in the surrounding peaks from here as well. I know if the air was clear, we’d be able to see much more.

We descend, and the loose gravelly stone is a challenge. The steep exposure doesn’t help our footing. We’re much slower than normal but the views are epic. We make our way back onto the main PCT and hit the Knife’s Edge, a famous section of trail. It’s incredible but the loose footing is still sketchy.

Finally we get back onto dirt and cross more picturesque meadows. We add this as another place to return to soon, this one actually reasonably close to home. As we descend we consult our maps on the remaining distance to the White Pass Cracker Barrel. According to FarOut, the gas station closes at 6 and showers and laundry close at 5. We’re going to have to hoof it if we want either of those things in addition to the package we’re getting there.

We both put in our audiobooks and fly. The scenery is still stunning, and we walk across an absolutely gorgeous section of trail. I realize that this is one of the last times I’ll be cruising along in an incredible place, trying to make it to a gas station in time to get a cold drink. This is likely going to be my last resupply on the entire trail. I take out my headphones (Dungeon Crawler Carl isn’t really appropriate for such a moment) and try to fully appreciate where I am and how far we’ve come.

We dip into the forest and are hiking along the White Pass ski area. We’re making good time, but we’re not fast enough and will likely miss out on showers and laundry. This is a bummer, since we haven’t done real laundry since my birthday and haven’t taken a shower in Washington yet. I dig through old FarOut comments and find an alternate people used to take down the road through the ski area. It’ll get us right to the Cracker Barrel, and maybe save us just enough time. We scurry along the shortcut and to the road. Pounding away on a gravel surface always hurts my feet but we’re both hopeful and push the pace. We make it to the gas station just in time; they tell us laundry is closing in 10 minutes. It’s quick work to throw in everything we have besides our rain jackets and sleeping pants. We shower, get our box, and choose a dinner from our re-supply. We have far too much food to justify buying anything.

The hiker area behind the gas station is busy and social. We charge our things and add more than a few items to the hiker box. Most people are taking their time heading north, hoping the fires will go out and the trail will re-open. We are not so optimistic and have family meeting us in Snoqualmie Pass in 4 days. Our plan is to hike there and in all likelihood, conclude our thru hike at that point. Its sad, and we’re more than a little downtrodden. Our bodies are tired though. Sarah has been experiencing an achilles issue that is getting worse and worse. She limps to our camp that night. I roll my ankle on the road and join her in limping. We talk about the possibility of continuing the trail from Snoqualmie, but at this point the alternates do not look good to us. We want to hike the PCT, and with this section in our backyard that opportunity will be available to us another year. We cozy up in the tent and vow to make the best of our final days on trail.

Your First $1,000 – Cushion, Card Debt, Then One Fund in a Roth IRA

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Send your first $1,000 to the earliest step you haven’t finished: a starter emergency fund, then any credit card balance, then a Roth IRA holding one broad fund. Collect your full 401(k) match from your paycheck along the way. It’s an instant return, and none of it comes out of the $1,000.

Investing the $1,000 first feels like progress. But with no savings, the next surprise bill goes on a credit card, and you pay card interest to keep the $1,000 invested.

Paying off a card earns a guaranteed return equal to its interest rate. On card accounts that were charged interest, that rate averaged 22.36% in August, according to the Federal Reserve. No investment promises that. For other loans, the interest rate decides whether to pay them off before investing.

Then comes the Roth IRA, a retirement account you fund with money you’ve already paid tax on. You can contribute if you have pay from work and your income is under the IRS limit. Its growth comes out tax-free after age 59½, as long as five years have passed since the first tax year you contributed for. What you put in can come back out anytime without tax or penalty, so a first investment there stays within reach.

To find your step, ask whether your starter emergency fund is full and whether you carry a card balance. Whatever the answer, collect any 401(k) match your employer offers, since it comes out of your paycheck, not the $1,000. A 50% match adds 50 cents to every dollar it covers, so raise your contribution now if you aren’t getting all of it.

Say you already get the match, your fund is $600 short, and you owe $400 on a card. Put $600 into savings, pay off the card, and open the Roth IRA with your next paycheck.

In the Roth IRA, buy one fund. Pick a broad index fund that tracks the whole U.S. stock market, or a target-date fund for the year you plan to retire, a mix of stocks and bonds that shifts toward bonds as that year nears. Skip single stocks, so the $1,000 doesn’t depend on one company.

Moving the money into the account doesn’t invest it. It waits as cash until you place the order, so buy the fund as soon as the money arrives.

To pick a brokerage for your Roth IRA, compare the best places to open an IRA.

Charles Porter Dies: ‘Finding Faith’ Producer & Actor Was 47

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Charles F. Porter, the actor and producer known for backing the 2025 film Finding Faith and playing the titular NASCAR phenom in ESPN’s Wendell Scott: A Race Story, died on Monday after a long battle with cancer. His passing was announced by his wife, Candice Tab-Porter. He was 47.

Porter was a multihyphenate in more ways than one. Born in the U.K., he went to college at Duke, where he played defensive end on the school’s football team. After a back injury derailed his NFL hopes, Porter dropped 65 pounds and became an actor.

In the early 2000s, he made appearances on The Parkers, Close to Home, Without a Trace, Bones and House.

In 2011, he landed the lead in Wendell Scott: A Race Story. The ESPN TV movie was based on the story of Wendell Scott, who in 1963 became the first and only African-American to ever win a NASCAR Cup race. Porter was the lead. Watch on Deadline

He was also the lead and a producer on Image Entertainment’s 2006 horror thriller Nailed. Additionally, he had a small part in STX’s Jennifer Garner vehicle Peppermint.

Last year, Porter produced Lifetime’s Finding Faith starring Paula Patton, Stephen Bishop, Keith David and Nadine Velazquez. He also had a small part in the film as a bartender.

Porter is survived by his wife, Candice, and two daughters, Juliette and Layla.

Nithya Raman Projected to Win Los Angeles Mayor’s Race, Defeating Karen Bass

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The city councilmember, who represents an industry-heavy district, ran an insurgent progressive campaign against the city's Democratic Party machine, unseating troubled incumbent Bass. One respected outlet has called the race.

Nithya Raman is projected to be the 44th mayor of Los Angeles, according to one outlet, unseating incumbent Karen Bass, a fellow Democrat who struggled with credibility as constituent anger surged over various municipal ills, including housing and affordability, the entertainment production exodus and her response to the Palisades wildfire. A win for the leftist City Councilmember, running as an outsider against the local party machine, would be a significant victory for the region’s progressive movement, which heavily skews toward younger voters who rent instead of owning their homes. Related Stories News Best Memes and TikToks After Midterms' Blue Wave, From Regina George to 'The Office' References Midterms 2026 DNC Vice Chair: "We Had to Hit the Bottom"

Independent election polling site Decision Desk H.Q., which partners with Ballotpedia, announced it was projecting a Raman victory, though other major entities such as NBC News had yet to call the race. Raman is currently up more than three percent, with nearly three-quarters of expected votes now tallied. The full count may not be in for a week or longer, as any mail-in ballots postmarked by Election Day are still valid even if they arrive by early next week.

During her election night party on Nov. 3 at the Greek Theatre in Griffith Park, Raman denounced the “cynicism” which “started to make us believe that L.A.’s problems weren’t just bad, but they were permanent.” She avowed that the city “is worth fighting for every single day of our lives.”

Raman, 45, is a Harvard- and MIT-trained urban planner who founded a L.A. homelessness nonprofit and served as executive director of Time’s Up, the ill-fated advocacy organization focused on workplace sexual harassment, before winning her City Council seat in 2020. The industry-heavy district spans the southern swath of the San Fernando Valley, from Sherman Oaks to Studio City, across the Hollywood Hills to the Eastside enclaves of Los Feliz and Silver Lake. It includes the Hollywood Bowl, the Hollywood Sign and NBCUniversal.

Through each of her campaigns, Raman, who was born in India and will be the city’s first Asian American mayor, has generated Hollywood support — particularly from its TV comedy scene. Donors to her mayoral race have included Tina Fey, Mike Schur, Mindy Kaling and Cord Jefferson. She’s married to Vali Chandrasekaran, a writer-producer who has worked on 30 Rock, Modern Family, My Name is Earl and The Four Seasons. (They have twin 10-year-old children.)

During their campaigns, both candidates said they’d back unlimited film and TV credits at the state level, and champion a federal tax incentive to make the U.S. more internationally competitive. Raman has attacked Bass’ handling of the issue, including her tardiness in appointing a film liaison to the industry. Still, despite her closeness to the industry, Raman’s record of advocating for Hollywood’s needs has been called into question, even as the city and state continued to experience its historic runaway production. (The effects of the entertainment free fall have been felt across the local economy.)

In office, Raman’s key policy focus has been on housing, first championing Measure ULA, known as the “Mansion Tax,” then criticizing the unintended consequences of the legislation once its ruinous impact on apartment construction became clear. She’s since pushed for reform. (The issue has been a recurring subplot on Netflix’s real-life drama about L.A. real estate, Selling Sunset.)

Bass, 73, was Los Angeles’ first female mayor and second Black mayor. A former community organizer turned veteran elected official, she spent years ascending to the top of California’s state assembly before spending more than a decade in Congress, representing a largely African American and Hispanic section of South L.A., which has been her political base. Joe Biden reportedly took her under serious consideration to be his running mate while he was the presumptive Democratic presidential nominee in 2020.

Bass has long maintained Hollywood ties. Her most prominent supporters in her mayoral reelection campaign included Jane Fonda, J.J. Abrams, Samuel L. Jackson and Anthony Anderson.

Raman ran to the left of Bass. Her campaign included a sit-down interview with the controversial leftist streamer Hasan Piker, as well as a fraught relationship with the Democratic Socialists of America. Once a dues-paying member of the organization’s local chapter, citing its commitment to housing affordability, she was censured by DSA L.A. in 2024 for not fully adopting its policy positions against Israel. In the mayoral race, the local chapter ultimately backed her, while the DSA’s national leadership challenged that endorsement.

Raman’s last-minute insurgent run against the local Democratic Party machine put her at odds with a bloc of her fellow progressives on the City Council, who had already endorsed Bass. The top sitting L.A. politicians to endorse her were County Supervisor Lindsey Horvath, who considered her own run against Bass and represents a swath of the Westside affected by the Palisades wildfire, along with the City Controller Kenneth Mejia. Now Raman, Mejia and newly elected L.A. City Attorney Marissa Roy, who’s a member of the DSA, constitute a triumvirate of progressivism at the highest reaches of City Hall.

The general election followed a heated primary race defined by the reactionary-populist run of the former reality TV star Spencer Pratt. He drew national attention for his high-energy campaign of grievance politics after he lost his house during the Palisades wildfire due to what he argued was government negligence.

Another key figure through the cycle has been the local billionaire real estate developer Rick Caruso, a center-right Democrat who lost to Bass in 2022. He declined to run again but has remained a prominent and influential presence in the city’s political conversation. Despite having fiercely criticized Bass during her term, including over her handling of the Palisades disaster (his own Palisades Village luxury shopping center was damaged), he announced in October that he would be voting for her.

Raman’s term will overlap with L.A.’s hosting of the 2028 Summer Olympics, a global spectacle offering the city an opportunity to reboot its tarnished image. The former Lucasfilm head Kathleen Kennedy recently took the baton to oversee the ceremonies after Peter Rice, who had been running the effort, became Netflix’s chief content officer.
